"I cannot!" she sobbed, her hands. "I insist on knowing the truth." He spokr- peremptorily. His determination not to appear harsh was slowly ebbing away under provocation. "Tell me if by confessing what you know of the matter you would implicate your father? " There was no answer. Grace leant back. sobbing in a hopeless, despondent manner. "Did he forge it ? " Sharply, almost fiercely, Dennis asked the question. "No! he did not forge it. I 'know he did not." "But you know who did?" He spoke with con- viction and growing bitterness. "I cannot say. Oh! spare me t"' she cried, throwing herself at his feet and clasping his knees. He made no reply, but looked with mingled Indignation and pity on her crouching form. For many minutes anger and compassion contended in his mind for supremacy. Then he said, speaking with a shade of gentleness : "How can I—a'mail of honour—act such a dishonourable part as to allow an absolutely innocent girl to be condemned as a forger, when I know that my wife possesses evidence that will acquit her? Surely, Grace, yo,. cannot be in fear of those miscreants who fre- quented your father's house, now that you are my wife. Why are you afraid of speaking the truth bravely? If the confession involves you in-dis- grace, I promise to stand by you and protect... you. You need not think that I shall leave you to• face the consequences alone. lam no coward, and—" But that sentence was never finished. The arms that clasped his knees had been gradually relaxina their hold. Grace sank to the floor in a deep swoon. An instant later Dennis was kneeling by her side, trying vainly to recall her consciousness. Man-like, he was hopelessly ignorant how to act in the matter. He tried vainly to recall what be had seen others do under similar circumstances. He chafed her hands and fanned her. But five minutes, then ten minutes passed, yet Grace lay with statuesque immobility, scarcely breathing, so it seemed to Dennis. Nameless fears came surging into his mind; and springing to his feet lie hastened upstairs and roused the maids. His hurried explanation that their mistress was ill, had fainted, caused them to descend in the shortest possible time. The doctor was quickly summoned, and Grace was carried upstairs just as consciousness was returning. Then followed long hours of suspense, as she lay hovering between life and death. In a vortex of misery end self-accusation Dennis paced the dining-room. The terrible fear that his wife would not survive, and that for her death he would be practically responsible, was never for an instant absent from his mind during those dreary hours of suspense.

CHAPTER XX. Twelve o'clock was striking as Dennis and Eric quitted the Trehernes' house and sauntered slowly along the street. Neither appeared inclined to talk; both walked in a preoccupied, measured manner, as if anxious that physical movement should not disturb the train of their thoughts. A nameless fear that Grace was in some way connected with the forgery, and dared not confess it, had taken possession of Dennis's mind. All through the long interview with Mr. Treherne this possibility had run parallel, so to speak, with the discussion as to the ways and means by which Mr. Treherne trusted Gwendoline's innocence might be proved. Eric's thoughts had taken somewhat the same course. As we know, he had suspected for some time that the forgery would be traced home to Ji the Tewsers; but Troup was the only man .o whom he had divulged these suspicims. Indeed, he had studiously avoided discussing a subject which, until this day, had in no way con- cerned himself. Now that his sister-in-law, for whom he had a genuine brotherly affection, stood accused of the crime he regarded the matter from a totally different point of view. He felt that he could no longer conceal from Dennis his belief that the Tewsers were implicated in the forgery, and that Mrs. Vane could probably throw some light on the subject. As he sauntered along by Dennis's side, thinking how he could best open the subject, he imme- diately realised the painfulness of the situation. "I suppose there's no use in dwelling on matters any longer to-night," observed Dennis, after a prolonged silent cogitation, bringing himself slowly to a standstill. "We shall know more to-morrow, and have a wider basis for our arguments." "Yes; we shall know more by this time to- morrow," concurred Eric. "But I say, Vane--" he added quickly—then stopped abruptly. "Yes; what were you going to say ? " "I scarcely like to tell you—but—hang it, old ' man, we have been friends for so long that I ought not to hesitate before telling you any mortal thing. Somehow, though, I feel as if I should like to shirk putting into words what is in my mind now. You'll take whatever I say in good part—l know that." Yes; especially if you won't beat about the bush. I like a fellow to speak out openly. It seems to me that there is too much reticence abroad. What is it you want to say ? " "It is this. Do you not think that perhaps your wife could assist us in unearthing the true criminal ?" Dennis shuddered and looked away from his friend. It was a glorious summer's night. Long after he remembered noticing how brightly the stars- were shining. "It is a curious fact," continued Eric, after a pause, and with growing composure, "and one that must not be lost sight of during the trial, that that cheque must have been extracted from a book you had when you were living with the Tewsers. I hope I'm not too outspoken; but you know I am the last man to willingly refer to unpleasant facts and—" "I know that," interrupted the other, "and all I ask is that you will speak up openly, and have no qualms about hurting my feelings. I know you're the last man to enjoy raking up painful details." "And listening to idle slander," added Hume. "But the knowledge that your father-in-law was not a reliable character has been forced upon me. Probably some of his associates were many degrees less reliable—men quite capable of committing forgery. Now, if we could obtain the names of a few of them, or gain information as to their ?resent confreres—without bringing your wife for- ward as a witness or implicating her in any way— the results might be satisfactory. I am only giving you a hint on the matter. The working of it out must lie in your own hands." "Yes I understand," murmured Dennis. "I will speak to my wife without delay. You can rely on my acting for the best. Good-night." Saying this, he left his friend abruptly. As he walked quietly home through the com- paratively quiet streets, the fact that the cool night air was refreshing and invigorating dawned upon him. Just then his thoughts wandered to Gwendoline, who, confined in a close, hot cell, must be suffering tortures, both mentally and physically. The hard bed, the loathsome ugliness of her surroundings—how distressful all this must be to one of her refined and artistic nature. Think- ing thus of her, he unconsciously quickened his pace. As he approached his home he saw that the lights were still burning in the drawing-room. He knew at once that Grace was sitting up for him. Latterly he had noticed that she was striving hard to endear herself to him, and was making one final effort to secure his love before their child was born. He thought of her with pathetic interest— but not love. For numerous reasons he regretted having to reopen that long-closed chapter of her life, which was so full of sadness. She was undisguisedly pleased to see him—so pleased, indeed, that feelings of compunction arose in his m•nd at the task he had undertaken. "You have sat up too late, Grace," he said, noticing she looked pale and tired. "I thought you would not mind it," she answered, almost shyly. "I have scarcely seen you all day." "Oh, no. I am very glad you sat up—YeTy glad. Especially as there is a matter I must talk over with you." "Yee ? " she answered interrogatively, pleased that he should think of consulting her. "I have heard some astonishing and unpleasant news to-day. I should have liked to put off mentioning it to you, but cannot." "What is it about ? " She looked down, and her fingers played nervously with the lace of her dress. "You have often heard me mention the Tre- heroes. Hume is engaged to the second daughter." "You mean the people that you met at Les Avants and that Aunt Hughes did not like." "Say, rather, against whom she MU/ 11101 t unfairly prejudiced. " "But what has happened to them ? " "The eldest girl, whose picture at the Royal Academy is the talk of London, has been arrested for uttering that cheque for fifty pounds to which my signature was forged." "How dreadful! " she stammered, turning ghastly pale. is dreadful. But you must not let the wws upset you so much. Her extreme pallor - frightened him. "Take matters more complac- ently," he added in a reassuring voice, though speaking earnestly. "It is greatly against my inclination to trouble you just now, but unfortu- nately there is no option in the matter." He paused, and, anxiously scanning her face, noted the effect of his last words. But no infor- mation could he glean therefrom. Hers was not the face of a woman capable of committing a crime, or of acting in a manner which demanded unscrupulous conduct. It was essentially a pure, though weak face. Not only in appearance but in character did she resemble her mother, who had died ten years before of a broken heart. As Dennis glanced critically at his wife he blamed himself for entertaining fears that she had forged the cheque. Moreover, the complete absence of defiance in her manner negatived all idea that she could be the criminal. Her gentle, helplessness exercised a curious spell over him. He felt disarmed. "Promise me, Grace, that you will not allow yourself to be over-excited or worried at the ques- tions I want to ask you? " He placed his hand on her shoulder. "Yes, I promise," she answered, trying to speak more firmly. "As to this forged cheque, it is evident to me that the book from which it was taken must have been in my possession when I lived with your leople ; so I want you to tell me the names of the irlen who frequented your father's house at that

worse."

"It is difficult—.l cannot remember," she mur- mured, looking away. His brows contracted, and a stern expression stole over his face as he continued: "I am afraid that amongst your father's friends were men of a discreditable type—men who uould not shrink from committing forgery. By telling us their names we ru;ght succeed in unearthing the true criminal. Miss Treherne is innocent; and, believing that you can assist in proving her innocence, it would be most dishonourable of me not to try and extract information from yon. Who had access to my rooms—tell me, Grace ? " "I—l—oh ! why do 9013 ask me ? " she faltered.

Cambridge University has just had a remarkable proof ui the mathematical genius of the Chinese. Some time ago much surprise was caused among mathematicians generally by the discovery among the papers of the late Sir Thomas Wade,of Chinese fame, I evidence that in the time of Confucius the Chinese knew an equation which only became known in Europe during the last century,when it was discovered by Fermat, and has since been known as Fermat's equation. But the Chinese ver- sion recently discovered stated that the equation did not hold with regard to certain numbers. This puzzled the mathematicians, and all efforts to solve the point have hitherto failed. Now, however, a young undergraduate of Trinity College,Cambridge, has demonstrated that the Chinese were right, and his s•Jlution is frankly admitted by the experts to be perfect. I understand the resu't is shortly to be published at Cambridge in an authoritative way.

A curious feat of engineering is being carried out in connection with the Church of St. Mary Woolnoth,in the City. As an alternative to the de- struction of the church,it was determined that the new station required by the City and South London Railway Company should be built underneath it. This novel scheme is now being carried out,and the fabric has been underpinned, so far without appar- ent damage. Below it a booking office and other adjuncts of a railway station are in course of con- struction.

The decision of the medical executive to prevent the practice of medicine by the unqualified assist- ant has had an unexpected result in certain dis- tricts. The assistant whom the medical man has 13P,en obliged by the recent ukase of the Medical Council to discharge has in some cases set up an establishment in the immediate neighbourhood where he •bends drugs and instruments, prescribe and dispenses medicines, and even visits patients and attends midwifery cases. Singularly enough,the assistant does not affend against the law unless he pretends to be a registered medical man, and thus no prosecution against him would succeed. Hence it is contended that as a sequel to its decision the MpAical Council should at once seek to strengthen the law to check these practices.

The idea of creating a mammoth Dock Trust for London with a capital of 50 millions gelling, is the outcome of tha two yeare' deliberation which the London Chamber of Commerce has bestowed on the metropolitan dock problem. If the scheme were to take shape it would proceed upon the principle of amalgamating all the dock compauies into one trust, and it will be at once seen that this is a mat- ter of infinite financial complexity, as regards the dock companies themselves and as regards the pub- lic who would be asked to provide the enormous capital required. Some of the dock companies are in favour of the principle for a genetal amalgama- tion, but say it is a shareholders' question. To tbe shipping trade the advantage is supposed to be a substantial reduction in the present burdensome charges. This project, it will be seen, merely sub- stitutes for several private companies one mach larger private company, and it in no respect meets the views of those reformers who aim at muni- eipalieing the docks or converting them into a pub- lic trust. Ido not believe that the promoters of this mammoth scheme will be able to agree with the companies as to the price to be paid for their re- spective interests, even though the stock at present stands at a big discount.

Admiral Boaanquet, emulating the example of Lord Charles Berestord, is turning a longing eye en the domain of " Scallywaggery " as a recruiting ground for the sea. But while Lord Charles thinks only or primarily of the Navy, the other admiral is chiefly concerned with the condition of the mercan- tile marine. Addressing the members of the United Service Institution on Monday, he put in a strong plea for a closer official communication be- tween shipowners, mercantile marine officers, and training and reformatory ships, with a view to in- ducing boys trained in those vessels to enter per- manently on a seafaring life. Admiral Bosanquet points out that in 1896 there were 27,765 foreigners serving on board British vessels, besides 27,911 Lascars. More alarming still is the decrease to which he calls attention in th 3 number of British sailor lads under the age of twenty. From 7,009 lin 1891, the number fell to 4,735 in 1896, and to 1,452 in 1897. Yet, according to a modest estimate, as many as 7,000 boys are required annually to re- place the waste in the seaman and fireman claps of British birth in the mercantile marine. In view of these somewhat startling !statistics Admiral Boman- quet's scheme seems entitled to serious considera- tion.

The Horniman Museum at Forest Hill. belonging to Mr, Ho: niman, the member for Falmouth, is a strange collection, the product e many years hunt- ing about the markets and Old curiosity shops of the world. Mr, Horniman hitherto has been very generous towards the public, as he has thrown his collection open several days a week ever 8111C8 he housed it some years ago in the southern London suburb. Be is now building for it a new house in the same locality, with the view of presenting it to the County Council, along with a park or recreation ground. There will be a free library in the grounds and also a clubhouse and lecture hall. The park will not be laid out as gardens, but for the healthful recreation of the masses. The fame of the mu,leum has long gone abroad among the people, as sinco Mr: Horniman threw the doors open it has been visited by half a million of sightseer-. No doubt the County Council will gratefully accept so hand- some a gift.

-- Mr. Birrell's allusion at Leicester to the rival Iri.h leaders as men who " sit scowling at one another like third-rate tragedians," expresses neatly enough the kind of impatient feeling which others on the same side of the Douse are watching the later evelopments of Nationalist differences. A con- dderable number of amendments have been tabled si view of the committee stage of the Irish Local inovernmen". Bill. One of the most important stands Gn the name of Mr. Dillion, who desires to see iwomen made eligible for election to the different councils. Mr. Vesey Knox gives notice of several new clauses, the object of which is to form the con- stituencies into councils, with power to construct or take over tramways.canals and other large works. The question of extending to other towns than those already specified in the bill the dignity ot separate County Council functions is raised in var- ious forms. Clonmel, Kilkenny, Wexford, Sligo and Drogheda are among the places mentioned az, suitable additions to the list.

ONE ON THE HIIMOURIST.—" Ihave brought you a bag of walnuts, thinking you would appreciate a change," said young Mr. Pompon to Snickers, the humourist. "A change?" replied the humourist, thoughtfully. "Yes, a change from chestnuts, you know."

WHAT THEY MEAL— When he says: "Oh, my darling! I worship you ! I adore you! I cannot begin to tell you how much I love you. You are more to me than anything in this world. I never lived until I met you. I can never be happy with- out you. Only say you will be mine, and I will be your devoted slave for ever !" It means: That he thinks she is rather a nice girl, and now that he is getting middle-aged, he'd better marry her and settle down. And that he has no doubt they'll rub along pretty well trgether, if she isn't too par- ticular about clubs and "nights off," and doesn't ever want her own way. When she says : " Yes ; Jack, Ido love you—a little bit." It means: That she loves him with all a woman's devotion and constancy. That she would marry him to- morrow, and gladly make any sacrifice for his sake.

THE LIGHTHOUSE LIGHT.

LIGHTHOUSE LADY IN DANGER.

, . <4 4 ~,z-5.-4,4-7.)-44

A true and very interesting story was the other day partially disclosed to a reporter of the " Bridgend Chronicle."

Anxious to ascertain, if possible, the facts surround- ing the case, the editor commissioned one of the staff to see the Nash Lighthouse-keeper's wife. Nash Light- house is situated on the rugged rocks of the Bristol Channel. On reaching it the reporter sawn lady, and In a few words unfolded what he had heard, viz., that a Mrs. Evans living there had had a narrow escape. " I am Mrs. Evans," said the lady, " and I have had what might be termed a miraculous escape." Mrs. Evans said that her husband,who is the principal keeper of the lighthouse, was placed there last March, having been removed from Caldy Island,Tenby. " Were you in good health when you came here?" " Well, I was weak, but not what might be called in ill-health. The air is bracing, but it made me ill. I think it is too strong for me. I had only been here a month when I was taken very ill, and my husband had to call in a doctor. I was ill for three months, and at the end of that period I was suddenly taken much worse. The doctor again came, and treated me with very great skill, but he informed my husband that it was of little use sending for him any more." "Your husband was very anxious about you P" " Yes, indeed •, I was suffering from severe indiges- tion, accompanied by a disordered liver." "Row do you account for your recovery then " asked the reporter. "Well, I can attribute it to one thing only. I was almost out of my mind with the severe and exoruoia- tin, pains in my head and racking of my inside result- ing from continuous indlgestion.My tongue was parched and gums swollen, so much so that I could not eat or drink,and if I did manage to eat anything I was racked with pain until it was vomited back. At this period, when I was at my worst,l saw an account in one of the panne of how many people had been cured by Dr. Wil- liams' Pink Pills for Pale Peopie.l sent for sorne.and had only finished the first box when I felt much better. Ere I had taken the second box I was able to get up from my hed,and on continuing to take them, and I am now able to go about my work. I can eat and drink well, si"P, and the racking pains of indigestion have for- saken me." Mrs. Stabbe, the good lady who nursed and was a constant attendant upon Mre. Evans during her illness. convinced the reporter that the ehange was due to Dr, Williams' Pink Pills, because,said she,l noticed a change in her before she had taken the first box. These are the plain unvarnished facts, as recorded to the reporter,and Mts. CHAPTER XXI.

"Grace has a daughter. Can you come ?" Mrs. Hughes read the telegram out loud to her husband with a glow of satisfaction. "Dear girl !" she exclaimed. "Of course I will go at once to her. Dennis did quite right to tele- graph. You must manage without me for a week or two, William."

"I will try to do so, my dear," he assented. "It would be very selfish of me not to spare you at such a moment."

Mrs. Hughes rose from the breakfast-table and rang the bell. She was in a flutter of happiness. Mounting guard over Dennis's young wife, new- born baby, and household generally was a mission after her own heart. She at once assumed to herself the position of grandmother to the new infant, and loved her in anticipation. How often she had longed for a little child of her own, on whom to lavish her powers of maternal love! Dennis's many good qualities had led her to love him dearly. But his worldly proclivities had shocked her on several recent occasions. He had received her much-thought-over advice and care- fully-considered rebukes with provoking calmness; and more than once she had noticed a merry twinkle in his eyes, when pointing out his faults and speaking in a most serious strain. But now that he had sent for her at this critical domestic juncture his shortcomings were at once forgotten, and she instantly began preparing for the journey to London. _ _

Meanwhile, Mr. Hughes had betaken himself to his study. He happened to feel in a mood for writing a sermon' and he was anxious to benefit from the inspiration before it evaporated. He had just indited the first few sentences, when his wife entered in that precipitate manner peculiar to herself.

"I find I must catch the ten-thirty train, and it is nine o'clock already " she exclaimed. "I came to tell you what a hurry I am in. Do, William, take some interest in my going away. There are the wraps to fasten up, and a present of vegetables, and some of our own butter, and a few new-laid eggs to pack." Mr. Hughes thrust his spectacles on his fore- head and looked at his wife with surprise. "I am writing a sermon, Maria, and I wish you would not come in so abruptly. It is so discom- posing. I was just in the middle of the opening sentence, and, as you know, so much depends on the first few sentences. Captain Cartwright rarely falls asleep when I am the if I succeed in gaining his attention at the beginning." Mrs. Hughes listened mechanically, without making any effort to comprehend what was being said. It was the wrong moment to hope to interest her in the opening sentences of a sermon. "Imagine our dear Dennis being a'father " she exclaimed in a delighted voice. "How he will dote on his little daughter !" "Can you approximate the time that you will be absent from home? " asked her husband.

"It will be three or four weeks at the least— until Grace is strong enough to come back with me for change. I have such a great deal to explain to you before I leave. Do put away that sermon and listen to what I have to say. I so rarely ask you to listen to anything. Come upstairs; I must begin packing at once." Mr. Hughes closed his desk, then uttered an audible sigh, as he rose and submissively followed his wife.

"I will tell you first about the Mothers' Meetings," she began, the moment she had reached her room. "You must arrange particularly for someone with tact to take them. Ask Miss Slycombe or Mrs. Griffiths to do so; if neither of them can, then write to Clara and ask her to come for the day and take the first. If she says no—" Mrs. Hughes stopped, and held up her best silk mantle and looked at it critically. Her thoughts were divided between its claims to being considered fashionable and a suitable person to "take " the mothers' meetings. "I could ask Miss Drew," said Mr. Hughes. "Miss Drew I " exclaimed his wife in astonish- ment. "Certainly not She does not believe in the Devil!"

"But, my dear, she would scarcely think it necessary to tell the mothers so." "She might, or she might not. But that does not alter the matter. It would be most foolish of you to place anyone in such a responsible position who does not believe in the Devil. If you take away he Devil, what will become of religion, William? ' "It is a difficult question to answer, Maria; one that neither you nor I need discuss at the present moment."

• "The meetings had better not take place if you cannot find a more orthodox person than Miss Drew. If you once allow her to have a voice in parochial management, your influence will be irre- trievably undermined." Then suddenly changing her tone to one of intense preoccupation, and regarding her mantle with renewed interest, she added: "I wonder if it could be raised on the shoulders." "What raised on the shoulders ? " asked her husband. "I was only talking about my mantle. But it is of no use my explaining these matters to you. You would not understand." "I like to see you in that mantle," he answered. "It reminds me of one my dear mother used to wear." "Oh i " It was clear by the manner in which Mrs. Hughes uttered the monosyllable that her husband's kindly-intended remark was not appreciated. "Don't forget," she continued with returning animation, "that Jemima, the black hen, is sitting on the gannet's eggs; and that the apples in the left-hand corner of the upper shelf in the store cupboard must be used up first; and —whatever you do—do not let John have the key of the beer-cellar. I shall never forget the dis- gracefal condition in which we found him on the deat the hay was carried. That reminds me of

! 174( am col the

something else. Don't let him neglect the seven little pigs that were born last week. "My dear, do not make my responsibilities heavier than I can bear. You seem to forget I have two sermons to prepare- for each Sunday." "it cannot take you six days to write two scr- ions. - iYoui must also keep • a sharp eye on Jme Suian. Be sure and do not let them have impany inAhe.kitehen. Suaan has two lovers at as present•mornenk, and she, tells me that she is jiironbled in her mind '-which to choose as a husband. I hope you will remember to pay sur- prise viaita-at the workhouse. It is the only way to find out if the inmates, especially the children, are properly treated." Bft. Hughes sighed. "I wish," said he, "that Dennis and his wife did not live so far away. Give them both my love and best, congratulations. How quickly you pack, Maria; I never saw any- one's hands move so nimbly as yours. You'll be able to catch the ten-thirty train quite easily." "Yes. But it is unfortunate that I have to leave so suddenly. Of course, I shall be quite prepared to find everything at sixes and sevens on my return." She spoke quite cheerfully, as if the fact of her presence at the Vicarage having the effect of main- taining order in the neighbourhood, generally, was a matter that no one could dispute, and which caused her peculiar satisfaction. To believe her- self a power in the land was her greatest pleasure in life.

A few minutes later the Vicarage carriage—an antiquated vehicle of curious build—was driven to the door by the much-suspected and occasionally erring John. Mrs. Hughes was waiting in the porch, rattling off final directions to the cook relative to preserving strawberries. Just then Mr. Hughes appeared, watch in hand, saying that there was no time to waste. No second reminder was required to induce his wife to take her place in the carriage. He immediately followed, and taking up the reins, gave them a shake, which Polly, the cob, had long ago learnt to recognise as a signal to start.

===== 0002601_18980325_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER XXII.

"Your wife must not hear any news of an exciting nature during the next few weeks, Mr. Vane," said Dr. Bolton, speaking authoritatively. "I am sorry to find that something is distressing her, which appears to have happened recently. Probably you know what it is, and I must ask you to do all you can to quiet her mind on the subject." "You may rely upon my doing so," was the answer.

"There is nothing in your wife's condition to cause anxiety at the present moment," continued the doctor reassuringly, noticing that Dennis looked harassed. "But it is absolutely necessary that you conceal from her all news of an unpleasant nature. Good-day." Dennis watched Dr. Bolton drive away with feelings of mingled relief and annoyance. There was an element of censure in the latter's remarks that jarred upon his overstrung nerves. But he was immeasurably relieved to hear that Grace was not in a critical condition, and that no serious consequences need be feared from the excitement of the previous night. He blamed himself greatly for having spoken with a straightforwardness that was cruel under the circumstances. His was a strangely complicated position. Honour demanded that he should strain every nerve to assist in proving Gwendoline's innocence; yet fate had willed that his lips should be sealed on the subject to his wife, though he suspected she possessed information which would prove strong evidence in the hands of the defence. Eric, he knew, must be relying on his having elicited facts, that either corroborated the suspicions that Grace knew who had committed the forgery or that negatived her having any knowledge on the subject. With a depressing sense of having failed to accomplish what was clearly his duty, Dennis arrived at Bow Street,. Mr. Treherne and Gwendoline were already in court; so also was Sir Edward Masters, who was attending expressly to obtain bail. Eric Hume was seated at the solicitors' table in the well of the court, and Dennis instantly joined him. "Virhat does your wife say?" queried the former at once, in a low voice. "Nothing." "Nothing! What do you mean? Surely she had something to say on the subject? " "I asked her several questions, but I could elicit no information in reply. Don't imagine I acted with lukewarm energy and did not do my best. Ae a matter of fact, I did too much." "How ? " asked Eric, looking up with surprise. "She was dreadfully upset at what I said, and fainted: Her child was born this morning."

... Eric looked aghast. "I hope she is in no danger—nothing serious," he faltered.

"No—happily. I tell you this, so that you will exonerate me from not having acted with sufficient decision. As it is now, the matter cannot be mentioned to Grace again for weeks--perhaps months."

"This is unfortunate—most unfortunate," replied the other. "I quite believed we could depend on your wife giving you information that would have made Gwendoline's acquittal a certainty: Now we must unearth evidence as best we can. However, I am sure you did all you could in the matter. In the end we are certain to get a verdict, but adjournments and a trial are hard things for a girl like Gwen to be called upon to face." "I wish I could have been of some practical assistance," said Dennis fervently. "You will be so, undoubtedly, if the magistrate accepts you as bail, which he is certain to do." Then Eric became absorbed in a conversation with counsel, to which Dennis listened, putting in a word from time to time. A pause followed, during which there was nothing to distract him from mentally revolving the terrible consequences of the case, should it be sent for trial and the jury return a verdict of guilty. His mind was in a whirl of apprehension; one moment he dreaded hearing the evidence that would be brought forward in support of the charge; the next he felt an intense curiosity to know facts which could form a basis for arguing that the case had no locus standi. The only word applicable to the present state of the case was mystery; and this mystery he was tired of trying to solve. He was absolutely certain, however, that no connection could exist between Gwendoline and the person who forged and uttered the cheque. After a while his atten- tion was attracted to the prisoner then in the dock, a brutal, degraded wretch, accused of maltreating his sick wife and young child. Dennis listened to the evidence of those who had witnessed this man's attack on helpless creatures with a feeling of growing indignation that clever, beautiful Gwendo- line Treherne should be compelled to stand next in the same place where this despicable specimen of humanity lounged with callous indifference. It was just twelve when her case came on for hearing. With a pale, calm face she took her place in the dock. Instantly all eyes were turned towards her. Blood surged to her pale cheeks, sad fierce, rebellious pride arose in her heart as she realised that whispered comments on her per- sonal appearance were on every lip. Uneducated and coarse-minded men and women were criticising her as she stood, quivering in every limb, yet drawn up to her full height. The moment was one of intensest humiliation.

Dennis Vane watched her with unfathomable pity. Had it been possible he would have sacri- ficed his life at that moment to rescue her from her present position. Mr. Treherne glanced nervously towards the dock, and with difficulty summoned an encouraging smile. Gwendoline, who had always delighted in the fascinating, natural merriment of her father's smile, was infinitely touched by its present sad- ness. But it would have been easier for him to be brave had he been standing in the dock himself. The case opened by a statement from the detective who arrested Gwendoline. He repeated her expressions of intense surprise when the charge of uttering the forged cheque was read to her. Then a server from Messrs. Tuke and Son, artists' colourmen, entered the witness-box and briefly stated that Miss Treherne called at the shop and prseented the cheque to be cashed, which, however, he had declined to do. Neat followed the evidence of Richard Musgrove, who swore that he had cashed the cheque for her at his shop in Mervale Street, Piccadilly. The proceedings were of the briefest. It seemed to Gwendoline that the case was barely opened before the magistrate adjourned it to the following Wednesday. Then Sir Edward Masters applied for bail. With feelings of keenest anxiety she listened to the discussion on the subject, and was intensely relieved when the magistrate decided to accept bail in two sureties of one thousand pounds, in the persons of her father and Dennis Vane. With a joyous feeling of recovered freedom Gwendoline quitted the dock and walked out of court by the side of her father.

COMPULSORY LAND PC RCHASE. By JOSEPH HYDER, General Secretary of the _ _ _

Land Nationalisation Soda

ARTICLE XX. If it Is for the benefit of the people, for the benefit of the agricultural class, but not of that class only, but of the whole community, that the State shou,d become the proprietor of all the land of the country, then it would be perfectly right that it should be done, and the landlords, r pon receiving fair compensation, would have no right to object Mn. GLADSTONE, at Hawarden Rent Dinner, 1889. There are some questions which many men will not examine because they dislike them,and which they dis- like because they have not examined them. Land Nationalisation is one of these questions. And perhaps the chief reason wily it has not yet received the atten- tion which its importance deserves is the existence of a common impression that it necessarily implies a policy of spoliation. Lord Dunraven voiced this belief some time ago when he declared that Land Nationalisation was "two long words signifying one short one—theft." Of course his statement was absolutely incorrect, but it drew a chorus of approval from Conservative editorst who are no wit more learned than are Conservative Peers in these matters. With the proverbial tenacity of life which ever characterises falsehood when it advantages those who promulgate it, this particular falsehood dies hard. It is much less prevalent now than formerly, but in many quarters it is still as fondly eherished as if it were a truth. A pithy saying of Abraham Lincoln is, however, encouraging. He said, " You may fool all of the people some of the time, and you may fool some of the people all the time ; hut you can't fool all the people all the time." Certainly the people have been fooled long enough on this question, by men who have been frequent and loud in their denunciation of land nationalisers as robbers of the worst type. But Ido not want to bandy personalitiPs. Calling each other robbers will in no way benefit either side of the controversy. Indeed, the introduction of the term "robber" generally has the effect of clouding the real issue. It inflames the passions without strengthening the reasoning powers. It embitters the discussion, and serves no good purpose at all. Moreover, it is not true

LANDIARDS ARE NOT ROBBERS. First, as to the landlords. No one can hate the system of orivate property in land more than I do. But my hatred is not of the landlords personally. The wrong is in the systcm, not in the individuals. Undoubtedly the system leads to the impoverishment of the industrious for the sake of the idle. It re-ults in the appropriation by one class of the wealth made by another class. But to stigmatise the landlords as robbers because of this iø quite unwarrantable. They cannot justly be likened to highwaymen or pirates, as is someltre,s so glibly done. by an energetic tint irresponsible and insignificant majority. The " common sense of most "revolts against such a comparison. Practically the nation is unanimous on this point. The leaders of both ,:oreat political par- ties are landlords, and between them they receive mil- lions of votes. If they are robbers, then the whole nation is equally responsible as an accessory to the rob- bery. Therefore, to propose to punish by impoVerish- merit the few who hold the land is really as unjust in in- tention as it is impossible to carry out. It is quite true, as Herbert Spencer says, that the present system VMS established by force and fraud. It is quite true that blood was the ink and the sword was the pen with which the first land titles were written. And it is quite true, that long after it ceased to be the custom to acquire land by means of physical force and violence, it was the custom to acquire it by the no less effective means of class legislation passed by Parliaments where landlords bad absolutely a monopoly of power, and where the masses were not represented at all. But those days are long since gone by.

THE ORIGINAL WRONG-DOERS are gone, too. They connot be got at. and they cannot be punished. The fact is,we • aye to right a wrong, not to punish a wrong-doer. The essential weakness of the non-compensationists' case is that they do not realise this. Surely it is not just that the present innocent holders of land should he made scapegoats and be pun- ished for the unjust acts of the!r predecessors in posses- sion. They have come ioLo po:session unconscious of any wrong,by the same meats exactly as owners of other kinds of property have come into possession, with the undoubted sanction of the whole nation, and the guar- tee of protection by the State. The buying of land has been, and still istregarded as quite a legitimate transac- tion—equally as much so as the buying of a house or any other product of labour ; equally legitimate and even more respectable. Indeed,the people are only new beginning to recognise the distinction between land and the products of land. For over 60 years the franchise has been p,.ssessed by the middle classes. For over 30 years it has been possessed by a considerable section of the working classes. B..fore the great Reform Act of 1832 it might have been ..aid that

THE LANDLESS MAJORITY were not responsible fer the laws and systems of property, as they had no voice in the government. But that cannot be said now. And not only had they hither- to accepted ane endorsed the system of private property in land as it exists in our own country, but they have also established it in all the colonies the have founded across the seas. The most democratic Governments in the world, those of the United States and our Austra- lasian Colonies, have even gone out of their way to en- courage the private acquisition of land. They have de- liberately parted with the national estate,and vast tracts of public land have been alienated in the most foolish A and reckless manner. Therefore for them to turn round ' and suddenly to declare, as a few do declare, that the buyer of land is in the position of the buyer of stolen goods, act must suffer the conseqaences of his mistake, , would be an act of national cowardice which they are . not at all likely to commit. The oft-quoted case of the man who has innocently bought a stolen horse does not apply. The State does not guarantee the title, as be- tween man and man, to any particular horse, any more than to any particular piece of land. It is understood that a man buys at his own risk. But it does declare that horses as horsea and land as land) are legitimate. subjects of ownership and sale. • The sudden conflcation of all horses would therefore be

I GROSS VIOLATION of rights of property. And the confiscation of all land would be a far greater one. In seeking the abolition of private property in land I therefore want to make two points clear. First : That the State has an absolute right to obtain the direct control and ownership of all the land within its borders. Second : That the present holders of land have a right to expect fair compensation when the land is so acquired. And when we come to facts as apart from the theories, we find that it is quite a common thing for the State to expropriate landlords and give to them compensation. Indeed, this is but the logical outcome of the well-known axiom of English law, thst all the land really belong: to the State, and not to• Individuals. Everyone is accustomed to speak of land- owners, but it is not correct to do so. Legal authorities may differ on other matters, but they are entirely, un- animous in declaring that, strictly speaking, every sub ject of the Crown merely holds his land as a tenant COf the Crown or State. The very name " freeholder" proves this. It obviously means one who holds his land free from any obligation to pay rent either in money or- in service. The central idea of the feudal System has never been discarded, although landlord Governments, have done their best to reduce its usefulness andpo make it little more than a theory. None the lees, it is important to bear Is in mind. Land-nationalisation, therefore, simply means the effective assertion of this atent State sovereignty. And fortunately we are able, to point to the fact that this sovereignity is already asserted from,time to time in c:rtaiu special circum— stances.

COMPULSORY LAND ,runcireen. At one time the State frequently took land from in— dividuals as a punishment, and many a titled landed family of the present day derive a princely income front - estates which were forfeited to the Crown by their former holders, who fell into Royal disfavour. But now-a-days land is never taken in that way. It is never taken except it is needed for some public purpose, and then its full (and too often much more than its full" value is taken in exchange. For instance, the State- compels the sale of land when it is needed for the mak- log of public improvements. If a street has to bo widened, or if a new road has to be made, the landlord may be obliged to sell the land that is needed for the purpose. If an area is over-crowded and unhealthy, be must sell to the public authority that is empowered to clear such insanitary places. To the School Board he must sell a site for the public schools. To the Board ef Guardians he must sell the site for a workhouse. To the Burial Board he must sell the site for a cemetery. When Glasgow wanted a new water supply from Loch Katrine and Liverpool and Birmingham from the Welsh hills, they were able to make landlords sell the nece.sary land. When the War Office wants a rifle range, or a drilling. ground, or an arsenal, or a fort, the same power is exercised. And if the admiralty wants a dockyard, the landlord is effectually reminded that tear° is no such thing in England as absolute private property in land, as there undoubtedly is in capital.

A. GOOD. PRINCIPLE. That is not a party question at all, so far aa the main principle is concerned. The Unionist Government of 188 enacted tha.: one acre allotments might be compul- sorily purchased for the benefit of the labouring popu- lation. The Liberal Government of 1894 enacted that five-acre hnkling might be compulsory hired. And Mr Gladstone at the Liberal Federation meetings at Man ehester in 1890 declared that while "the Allotment Act was ineffiuient, it yet contained the good prit that where land was needed in the judgment of a potent public authority, it might be purchaeed wiz tbe owner desired it or not." And not only has railway the power to acquire land by compulsion, the Church of England has a similar power to acquil church sites—a privilege possessed by none of the otl religious denominations, although they most need - Thus I claim that the essential argument of lac I Dationalisers is clearly established,aud that the right the State to acquire all the land.on equitable terms, ft° placed beyond dispute. For, it be just to acquire a Par It cannot he unjuq to acquire the who upon the ear terms.

LADIES' LETTER

My Dzere NELLIE.

The milliner's shop window look very attractive on the account of the wonderfully bright of hue are the new hats. AL the first glaixe it seems as if no one colour predominated in the gay show, but a more careful scrutiny will p.ove that although the rather trying, mauveish tone of blue known as pervenche, or periwinkle, is the colour of the season, it has important rivals in rose-pink, apple and grass greens, and red. The last-named hue, however,will probably vanish when the cold winds are over and the suns gains strength, and its suc- cessor will then be, I imagine, a rather deep shade Of petunia.

here is one shape beyond nil others to be seen -on the heads of fashionable women just mow. It is nearly round, of very moderate size, with a low crown that is almost entirely hidden by the trimm- ings, and a brim that is lifted on the left side by a full wreath of flowers or folds of velvet or silk which extends round to the back of the head. The hat is put on well forward, and is in reality set quite straight on the hair, but the mass of trimm- ing under the brim gives it an appearance of being titled to one side. A chenille and velvet hat of this description looks well wreathed with roses and draped with soft waves of black net, and a diamond star holding a black and green aigrette.

Girls of from four to eight are still wearing the straight frocks, out in empire fashion, and fitted either to a yoke or short empire bodice. They look well made in cashmere, serge, or soft, thin Woollen textures, with ruching of ribbon or of frayed silk, and yoke of fancy embroidered lawn. The collar-shaped arrangement round the yoke is made on stiff union or canvas, and is of irregular ,IlhaPe, the right side folded, the left out lip, and "limbed with a rosette of silk or ribbon. A very dressy little mantle, suitable for a young matron, has the body portion similar to a Russian blouse, and worn with a belt. In place of the Ordinary sleeve there are cape-like pieces, which fib over the shoulders and fasten down to the blouse, these in front leaving a space for the arms to pass through. Trimmed with jet and feathers, this is is very useful model for grey cloth, or iseireotive in silk or moir‘ with a ruche of gauze or grenadine • and jet passemonterie, with motifs .forming the corners

%n the tailor-made class of gown, skirts misers] longer than they were, and ,barely escape the ground all round, and fit perfectly tight round the hips. Flounces of the circular type have not been adopted by the tailor to any great extent, although they are seen here and there, but unmy of the skirts have extra slope in the dower part of the gores, and the trimming is arranged to suggest& aoanty circular flounce. Skirts with all the fulness ,tied back by tapes., and allowed ,to fall in two godet pleats, are the most graceful, but so closely are the tops cut, that there is only sufficient to make a very tiny little box-pleat on each side, or to arrange a few gathers. The tapes are altogether more adaptable than the elastic running round, as the strings may be tied more or less tightly, according to the petticoats worn, and it is possible to adjust the skirt in a be- coming fashion. The latest notion in the way of trimming on the skirt of a dress is the one-sided arrangement of jet passemeuterie on a violet cloth, one that is cut With the slight train now so fashionable, as in the third sketch. The .coersage and sleeves are of Parma violet silk muslin, tucked and trimmed With narrow black pioot lace, while the sides of tke bodice are solidified and strengthened by bands of handt.oine, yet not heavy, jet passementerie. At the throat is a long bow of mauve chiffon, centred by an oval slide of Parisian diamonds, and tho.ceinture is black satin. A great many spot silk bodices are made up with cloth skirts, very lalueh in blouse fashion, but very slightly baggy in front, and not at all behind. The sleeves, too, Pt smaller and more coat-like every week, though In thin materials they are amplified at the top. The very pretty long pointed shoes worn at the M.rst Diawing Room of the season have turned our attention to the all-important question of foot coverings. We are certainly falling much into the Iktnerican notion, with the result that the modern .trt is shown off to greater advantage than was .1,41 fashion years ago. For all dressy occasions aros would seem to be worn, almost to the exolu- ro°4 of boots, which many women only don for ugh wear in the country. Your loving .JENNIN.

an-tha sixteenth century, feathers were mostly em- -ployed in the manufacture of fans, and these fans did not fold. Early in the ,eighteeth century fans were of enormous proportions. Indeed,so huge were the fashionable ones that Sir Roger 'Coverley declared he would allow "the profits of a windmill" to the widow of whom he was the suitor,"for her tans." FlaVO a 4guare of muslin reads, on a hot plate and spread the linseed meal quickly on the muslin, making an even thickness. Then dip the iron spoon into hot water and rui:Oit over the linseed meal; and fold the muslin securely. If the poultice has to be carried upstairs, put it between two hot plates. After applying the poultice, cover it with a piece of oil silk te keep in the moisture. IMITATION PRESERVED GINGER.—Strip off the stringy part of garden rhubarb, and cut the stalks into pieces about two inches long. Put them in a saucepan with five pints of water, one pound of sugar, and two table- spoonfuls of ground ginger. Boil slowly for twenty minutes, set the preparation aside, and later on boil again half an hour, and repeat the process a third time. Make a thick syrup by boiling tzgether one cupful of water. one pound of loaf sugar, and one ounce and a half of ginger, for every pound of stalk, and when cold put the preserve in jirs, and rAver closely. It will be ready for use in a fortnight. One pound of rhubarb will make one pound of pm oe. - -

Arbitration proceedings in connection with the purchase of property by the Warrington Corpora- tion to enable them to continue the widening of Bridge-st. were commenced at the Lion Hotel, War- rington,on Tuesday morning. The property in dis- pute is the "Feathers" Hotel,Bridge-street,and two cottages and shop adjoining, which belong to Mr, Arthur Wilson. The arbitrator for the Corporation is Mr. William Wilson, of Manchester, and for the claimant Mr. James Clowes Sutton. Mr. Thomas Wainwright,of Liverpool,is the umpire. The counsel for the Corporation are Mr. Sutton and %Ir. J. K. Bradbury, barristers, of Manchester (instructed by the Town Clerk), and for the claimant Mr. W. F. K. Taylor, Q.C. London (instructed by Mr. Arthur Browne). The claim is for £16,000 for the Feathers Hotel and for three small cottages adjoining, and comprising an area of about 636 square yeards. At the Chancery trial he gave the tenants of the cottages notice to leave because he wanted fresh tenants. They had not been let since. The house wee not tied, and there was no agreement to take beer from Meomrs. Greenall. As a fact, however, beer was got from that brewery. 1i paid the rates on the house. The sole rate was £lO 2s. 81. and the improvement rote £9 12s. per annum. Hit father got all his beer. lexcept bottled beer and stout, from Greenall's in 1896 an 1895. The tenant had the option of becoming tenant of the new hotel proposed to be erected at the rear of the present building. That was a stipulation of the present tenant when he took up the tenancy. Arthur Brnwne, the claimant's solicitor, was next called and said he acted a geed deal in licensing matters for the Licensed Victuallers' Association, Messrs. Green. all and Whitler and Messrs. Peter Walker and Sons. He formerly acted as solieitur for ;he claimant's father, and was consulted by the latter with regard to giving the whole of his property to his son Arthur, who was his favourite son. He (witness) persuaded him not to do P n, as he did not think it fair to the other children. Casual conversation took place between the deceased and himself about the matter. Eventually on his advice, the deceased agreed to take £1,500, that sum being arrived at as £5OO for each of the other children,but the late Mr. Wilson would Persist in making a deed of gift of the cottages. No valuation was made of the public• house when it was agreed that Arthur Wilson should pay £1,500. Arthur Wilson also undertook to pay off the curr -nt debts of the houge. which amounted to £270. Samuel Wilson died on sth January, 1897, he having advised him on his will. Another solicitor, Mr. Jenkins, wag called in to make hid will the day before his death. The will appointed Mr. Jenkins and Mr. Johnsen Wilson executors. They brought an action to upset the deed, and after the plaintiffs' case had been partly heard the Vice-Chancellor suggested a settlement remarking to the plaintiffs' counsel that his client had over-reached himself. A. consultation took place and another £l,lOO was raised without a moment's hesitation in the Court. After the action the present ten- ant took the house. The house was net tied. The popu- lation of Warrington was increasing rapidly. There were 87 fully licensed houses, and only nine free lenuals.—By Mr. Bradbury : It was true that by an agrearnent,dated August 24th, 1894,Arthur Wilson was to have the option of'purchasing the whale of the premises, house, stables, and cottages for £3,000. That was the result of one of the conversations that parsed between him (witness)and

Samuel Wilson. In re-examination by Mr. Taylor, he geld the agree- ment of 1394 was drawn up on the same gr iund ae the subsequent one, namely,that Arthur Witsou was the de- ceased's favourite son. Alfred Eddowes,aerated water manufacturer, said he bad known the Feathers Hotel for 20 years. In the lifetime of the late Samuel Wilson he tried several times to buy it. He offered £7,000 foe the whole of the pre- mises. In his view it was worth more. He made the same effer to Arthur Wilson. He had made no inquiries about the business,but he judged from its position that it would be a good speculation. Alfred Starkey said he had been the tenant since the 31st August,lB97. Be tried to get a lease,but could not on account of the proposed alterations. There was not much business when he first went there, but it was in- creasing every week. SECOND DAY'S PROCEEDINGS. The proceedings were resumed at the Lion Hotel, on Wednesday. Mr. John White was re-examined by Mr. Taylor. He said the practice adopted for arriving at the value of public-house property in this neighbourhood was to take one-eighth of the takings. Brewers,bowever,gener. ally reckoned 14 or 20 years' purchase on the net profits which were one-third of the gross takings. Alderman Sutton was the next witness called. Re said he was an auctioneer and retate agent,and had a good deal of experience in telling and valuing properties In Warrington. Bridge-st. was the most important of the four principal streetset being the only approach from ails parts of Cheshire. The side on which the Feathers Hotel was was considered the most valuable. He had been appointed in the case of at least three-fourths of the properties in the four principal streets either to sell or value. Be had a good knowledge of both annual and saleable values. The properties in these streets were continually increasing in value—in proportion to the in- crease of the population. There was a deinaed for premises in Bridge-mt., but it was impossible either to find shops to let or on sale. Be had :sold and valued a good many licensed premises. His valuation of the Feathers Hotel was £lBO for the annual value of the hotel itself, and the saleable value on 30 years' purchase £5,400. The annual value was estimated according to the yearly rental of similar licensed houses in Warring- ton, and the profits which were made at such houses. The method he usually adopted—and he found it a fairly reliable one—was to fix the yearly rental at 124 per cent., or one-eighth of the estimated gross takings. He estimated that the takings in a position as good as that should be £2B a week, or £1,450 a year, which calculated on that basis would give £lBl a year. For the stables and the joint use of the pump in the yard he put down £B5 a year, and the saleable value at 25 years' purchase at £1,624. He put the site down —3OB yards at £3 a yard, £924, and the buildings £7OO. There were eight stalls, which at Si. 6d. a week each would bring in £52 a year. Then there were carriage sheds and workshops over, which were very valuable to a tenant. In addition there was a saving of £lO a year in the use of water, of which there was ample steeply from the pump. Taking £52 for the stable,' it only left £l3 a year tor all the other buildings and the use of the water. He considered that £65 was a very low figure,and was a less figure than they could get such premises for in such a convenient situstooneu any part of Warrington. He put the net annual value of the cottages £3l acd the saleable value at 20 years purchase £620; value of the land at 120 yards at £4 a yard £4BO, and the buildings £l4O. If £lOO or £l5O were spent on them the cottages could be made habitable, and there would be no difficulty in getting £4O a year clear. The total annual value of the property was therefore £278 and the total saleable value £7,644, which was equal to £ll 18s 91 per square yard tor the whale of the property including the value of the license, or £7 ss. per yard de- ducting the value of the licence. He thought this was very much below the average price which properties in this street would always fetch, it was about one-third of the average value per yard. He also put down £764 for compulsory sale at £lO per cent, and £450 as com- pensation to tenant of stable. Zeo., for disturbance,mak. ing a total valuation of £8,858. Cross-examined by Mr. Bradbury : He should not say that the site of the Feathers was the most valuable part of Warrington. He admitted that the sites of property in other parts of Bridge-street would be worth more. He had taken the average, and his estimate was less than the average.—What sort of trade do you assume is done by the Feathers. Is it a through trade for Cheshire? —Yes, a good eeneral trade.—Do you suggest it does a trade because it is on the route from Cheshire to Lance- shire T—To a certain extent. Yes.—What proportion of the trade do you put down to that P—l can't answer that.—You have put your valuation partly on the basis of this through route fro m Cheshire to Lancashire, and that makes a material difference in the takings P—lt makes the house more valuable, and it should take more money.—Don't try to fence the question Mr. Sutton.— I will say" Yes" then.—How many public-houses are there between Ines bridge and this house P—Five on the opposite side, and one on the saute side. In answer to further questions witness said he consi- dered that the part of Bridge-street where the hotel was situated was better than any other part of the town for trade. He knew of his knowledge what most of the pisblic-tiouees in Warrington were doing. It was not usual to deduct the outgoings in valuing licensed pre- mises. It was usual to tette a number of years' purchase en the actual rent, and he had done so. If £lB a year were deducted he should put it on 33 years' purchase. He was of opinion that the cottages were in a sanitary cendition.—Do you know that the floor is lower than the ground ?—Yes.--Row would you remedy that to make them sanitary 7—The way I would remedy it would be to pull the whole property down and rebuild it.—Why didn't yowl tell me that a quarter of an hour age ? —I would do that if I was compelled to do so. Mr. Peter Wright, the managing director of Messrs. Peter Walker and Sons, was called to give evidence with regard to a letter which was put in on the previous day, in which an offer was made by the brewery company for the hotel. He asked that the amount mentioned in the letter should be kept private. A discussion took place between the arbitrators and counsel, and it was suggested that the matter should be dealt with in private. Mr. Bradbury objected. Mr. Bradbury : It is in the interests of he administra- tion of justice. This is not a public court, but it is a court where one witness ought to be treated like an- other. The effect of the letter was "If I can get a license transferred to there permanently, I am going to eive a certain sung." He contended that that had no effect on the value of the property. At the Feathers the two front rooms were furnished with deal tables, so that a stranger would never go there the second time. He had been in there several tirnes,and had never seen any farmers there, but only a few personal friends. It was only patronised by people living within 300 sr 400 yards. The trade de- pended to a great extent upon the ability and energy of the person who occupied the house. Since the present tenant came the house had improved, and it might be further improved,but money would have to be spent on it in furnishing it. If the house were renovated and made more like a modern house there was no doubt more business might be done. He assessed the house, stables and yard at gross £7s,rateable £64. I

- Tars questions which seem to The National l have most deeply move Liberal Feclera- d the dele- tion. gates at the National Liberal Federation Liberal Federation held at Leicester this week are two in num'-ier, one of them relating to a domestic and tho other to a foreign affair. The subject of domestic interest relates to the reform of the House of Lords and in this connection Mr. JOHN LLOYD-GEOHIIE, M.P., seems to have most accurately expressed the opinions of the assembled representatives of Liberalism. Mr. LLOYD-GEORGE, speaking as a Welshman, was entitled to appropriate one crumb of comfort from the fact that his own countrymen were in no way responsible for the unreformed condition of the House of Lords. It is not with the good will of the Celtic fringe " that the Peers are left in possession of the veto upon legis- lation which is used only when the Liberals are in power. It is England and England alone which needs conversion on this subject. But, as he pointed out, the present Tory Government is itself . providing arguments which will help to make the demand for "ending or mending" irressistible.. The one respectable plea for the preservation of the House of Lords as a legislative authority is. that, as a second chamber, it is a check. upon hasty or rash legislation. This is. ithe theoretical justification of the existence of the, House of Lords, and it may be admittcd thatif the practice really squared with the the theory all proposals for the reform or abolition of that, House on the ground of its anomalous consti- tution would meet with a very meagre support in the country. Practical men trouble themselves very little about the illogical methods by which the ranks of the Peers are recruited from time to time. What they do trouble themselves about, however, is the utterly ineffective and,, indeed, farcical manner in which the House of Lords exer- cises its revisory powers upon legislation. These powers are only used when Liberals are in office. No matter how crude or even scandalous the measures propounded by a Tory Cabinet may be they are accepted and endorsed almost without criticism by the hereditary chamber. The Agri- cultural Rating Bill was a monstrous example of class legislation. It is estimated by Mr. LLO GEORGE that the Rating Bill added to the value of the estates of members of the Government which framed it to the extent of £60,000 a year. There is a strong Tory majority in the House of COEII.• mons, but this Bill could not be forced through the popular House, which is responsible to the electors, except by a liberal use of gagging and guillotining resolutions. How did the Lords act when this little scheme for the spoliation of the ratepayers for the benefit of a priveleged class come before them ? Did they deal with it as they dealt with the Home Rule Bill and many another Liberal measure, and challenge the Government to appeal to the country? Not at all. As Mr. LLOYD-GEORGE aptly puts it, these guardians of the public inter- est were on th occasion too busy in lining their own pockets with the ward's money to perform their trust, and passed the Bill by a large ma- jority. Other schemes of class plunder have found

ft place on the statuts books through the instrumentality of the present Government, and not in one instance has the House of Lords Proved itself to be tho guardian of the public in- terests. "A bone," says Mr. LLOYD-GEORGE, "was thrown to tho watch-dog while the house was robbed. Two millions to the landlotds, £BOO,OOO to the parsons, £750,000 to the Irish landlords ; when, does it come from? It comes from the fund set apart for the poor, the broken, the infirm and the aged. The landlord and the

Parson have broken into the poor-box; they have

robbed the poor and they will pay the penalty if there is any honour in England at the next elec- tion." This pungent exposure of the short-com- ings of the House of Lords evidently voiced the Sentiment of the National Liberal Federation, and the manner in which the question of tlae Peers Veto was received at Leicester is one more indica- tion that the obstructions of the hereditary cham- ber will be faithfully dealt with when the Liberal Party once more regains power. The question oi foreign policy was referred to A by Mr. JOHN MORLEY in a powerful speech de- livered at the mass meeting held at Leicester on Wednesday. In Mr. MORLEY'S opinion the foreign relations of the country are in a too criti- cal condition to permit of merely party criticism. lie does not attempt to disguise the fact that there are serious dangers ahead, and all he asks is that the Government should formulate a policy With regard to both China and West Africa that Will meet with the approval of the countfy geterally. This is a reasonable demand. No )3rltish Government can deal firmly and con- listently with foreign powers unless it has the full weight of public opinion behind it, and the

Bupport of that public opinion can only be Obtained by a sound national policy. As far as is concerned the country is left in doubt as t° which leg our Government wishes to • stand up-

_ °II. It was at first announced that the only in-

terest of this country was to be found in keeping all "open door" for trade in China—in other words, le asserting the principle of free trade with the Nnesc. That line of policy mot with general aPProval. But more recently our own Govern- Out has displayed a disposition to join in the service upon Chinese territory. There has ap- Parently been an attempt to bring a portion of China within our sphere of influence, with the almost inevitable result that other Powers have been stirred into renewed activity. It is clear, therefore, that Mr. MORLEY can not be accused of being over-critical when he asks the Government to define its foreign policy.

A brief debate upon the merits Compazisol. and dJaisrit-4 of the educational system that took place in the House of Commons on Tuesday should be followed with interest by all who are in- terested in education. It arose upon a motion by Sir Jolla LUBBOCK stating " That it is desirable to assimilate the provisions of the English Edu- cation Code, as regards class and special subjects, to thossia the Scotch Cods of 1897." On the face of it this motion seems to be calculated to further the instruction of the young, yet it was opposed by some of those whose zeal for education is be- yond question. The reasons for this opposition are in the highest degree suggestive. It appc 1:s that What is suitable for Scotch schools is not suitable for English, for the two reasons that children re- tain longer at school in Scotland than in this cone trY and that the teaching staffs are, as a rule, ' more efficient in the northern Kingdom than in 111. ngland. According to Arr. Yoxat,t, an authority, 14 Scotland there is a higher percentage of adult tAlehers, a larger proportion of whom are certifi- 4ted, the school attendance is better, and the Scholars remain till a later age. Sir WILLIAM H B.a- -I:l3lm:pointed the moral of the discussion when he d that in the opinion of those most competent to 4,11(10 we have not got the material or machinery in_ttlis country for 'giving to English subjects a ;cora education. In view of this, he asked, what the arg,ument so of ten heard lately that the i:.°arcl Schools were given too high an education. rs concluded that education was better in Scot- :lid because the Scotch people thoroughly under- °od the practical value of it, and he urged the tt,eecl ef a scheme of secondary education in Zingland.

The discussion at the Town Council meeting upon Councillor H. Roberts' motion brings out one fact :terY Clearly, namely, that the Gas Committee has been in the habit during the last few years of %imaging its affairs more as though it were a private concern rather than a branch of the Corporation. The financial transactions which V*onncillor Roberts referred to appear to be extra- Ordinary enough in themselves, but the most extra- trdinary thing of all is the apparent ignorance of the Coun,!il as a whole of the business of the Gas Collolittee. The report asked for in the motion lalould be provided without any needless delay, if °MY for the purpose of supplying members of the Council with the informatitin that is necessary to 'enable the to understand tbe position of the gas department.

Outside the town there will, according to the nominations, be contests at Penketh, and Poulton- with-Fearnhead. At Penketh, the retiring member Mr. Dignum, is opposed by Mr. R. B. Matthews, but it is not expected that there will be any change in the representation. Mr. Dignurn has represented the township for a considerable number of years, and his past services will commend him to the electors. The contest at Poulton-with-Fearnhead will be of an interesting character. The three candidates for the one seat, Mr. H Thornton, Mr. W. Bennett, and Mr. CL Mather, are all well-known local residents. Mr. Thornton, however, possesses one special advantage. He has been chairman of the Parish Council for two years, and during that period has been active and successful in improving and developing the district. As the representative of the township on the Highway Board he has closely and effectively looked after the interests of the locality,and these past services give him a claim upon the electors which will probably carry him to the top of the poll.

On Tuesday afternoon an employ 6 of Mr. Cotton, engineer, Scotland-road, was working on a bhed at Messrs. Bishop and Co.'s wharf, on the banks of the Mersey, at Bridge-foot, when he fell into the water, a distance of about thirty feet. Fortunately, the tide was at the full at that time, and he escaped injury. Ile was got out of the water by some work men. SPIRITUALISM. Miss S. Scott, of Rochdale, was the speaker at the services on Sunday and Monday. INDRPENDENT LABOUR PAR'S:.

At the weekly meeting of the above, held in the Druids' Hall, on Sunday evening, Mr. J. Bell gave an address on " Christianity and Socialism." Mr. Scott occupied the chair, and read a portion of the Epistle of St. James. The speaker said they must distinguish between creeds, religion, and Christ- ianity, and to obtain a true conception must go back to Christ himself. After reviewing His life, teaching, and acts, and having drawn from them , His essential precepts, the speaker went on to show I how Socialism and Christianity ha-monized. There was no antagonism between them, but Socialism was the attempt to carry out in social and indus- trial life the principles of true Christianity. An I interesting and spirited discussion followed the address, and after the speaker had replied the meet- ing closed. Tx MILTON SAYS SUSTAIN I Loss OF £4,000. Damage estimated at from £7,000 to £B,OOO was caused by a fire which broke out at Avenham-street Mill, Preston, early on Monday morning. The build- ing was formerly a large spinning mill belonging to Me,srs. Herrockses and:Jackson, but was partially destroyed by fire in 1879, and was not reconstructed The remaining portion of the old mill, which was built forty years ago, was tenanted by a variety of tradesmen,and in that portion where the fire origin- ated were a joiner's shop, a slipper monufacturer, the Milton Rays theatrical scenery, a lithographers workshop, and bakery. The fire, though small when the brigade arrived, rapidly gained ground and burned its way from the third storey to the top of the building—a six-storied one, and the building was entirely gutted by four o'clock. The Milton Rays are losers to the extent of £4,000. MAZAWATTEE MAZAWATTEB Dkuctous MAZAWA.TfER FRAGRANT, lIIAZAWATTSM REFRESHING. MA.ZAWATTEM ECONOMICAL. MAZAWATfRa Bold by HENRY MILLING and Co., LIMITED, 5 Horsemarket-street, and Corporation Buildings. Role Wholesale and Retail Agents for Warrington. WARRINGTON FIELD CLUB. On Friday evening last at the annual and ento- mological meeting of the above club there was a good attendance and a beautiful display of insects arranged by various members. Afuer these had been inspected Mr. William Mounfield described his ex- hibits, and made some remarks on the entomologi- cal work of the past year. Mr.Womersley then read some brief notes on the habits of certain ichneu- mons, insects related to the wasps, whose practice of piercing the soft bodies of caterpillars and de- positing therein the eggs which on becoming larva prey on their renders them a vexation of spirit to the ardent lepidopterist. Mr. Womersley's observa- tions were deemed to possess a permanent value, and he was asked to deposit a copy of his notes amongst the official records of the club. Mr.B.Fair- clough exhibited some specimens of eats, and gave a lucid account of some recent experiments and re- searches intended to secuse greater weight and better quality of grain. Mr. Greening exhibited two bottles of interesting Malagasy spiders,Gasterocantha formosa, which have such curiously-shaped bodies that they look like prickly knobs of dead bramble rather than what they are, cruel natural traps for unsuspecting flies. The secretary's report was then submitted and adopted, the .usual votes of thanks were accorded. Mr. J. Shaw Green was elected chair- man, and Mr. A. J. Jolley was re-elected secretary. PM IN CHRIST CHURCH WESTON POINT.

The Runcorn Fire Brigade was called out shortly after two o'clock on Saturday morning to a fire which had broken out in the tower of Christ Churcig, Weston Point. The fire had obtained a fait hold of the tower before the brigade arrived on the scene. There was a good brefze, and this hplp^,l the flames to susickly en- velope the top part of the tower. As soon as the firemen arrived at the church they commenced operations, an abundant supply of water, as the church is surrounded by the locks and the Ship Canal, was at the disposal of the firemen. A loud crash was heard, which afterwards was found to have been caused by the falling of the church bell and clock, which were smashed to atoms. The flames had become master of the situation, and the men endeavoured to confine the bingo to the tower, which was completely gutted. The interior of the tower was destroyed, and considerable damage done to the surPlices,alms-plate,and other furniture. The origin of the fire in a mystery. During the previoul afternoon several men were engaged in the church,and when they left at 6.30 in the evening even-41f ng was ancarentlyall right. The damse'e,•vhich is estimated at £soo,is covered by insurance. IstForm %Hon of the fire was received at Runcorn by the police through the telephone, the rnes- sage being sent Ciy Louis Rosenberg, who had been informed of the affair us' a lollt tenter,named William Savage.
