S ViTAGE ASSAULT ON A. FR,ODSHAM SCHOOLMISTRESS.

SEQUEL TO CANING A GUILD.

--- - A married worn ,n named Elizabeth Antrobus cf Fred- sham was chargad on a warrant at the Frodsham Police Court on Saturday,before Messrs. A. Thomas(chairman) and J. Ockleston, with assaulting Mary Roberts,at Frod- sham on Tuesday, the 15th day of March. Mary Roberts, headmistress at the National Schools. Frodaham, stated that on Tuesday morning 15th March, MS she called a number of children out who had been absent from school. Amongst those who came out was the prisoner's girl who said "if you cane me my mother will came to you this afternoon. She did not cane her then, but she was obliged to do so about 20 minutes to 12 for disobedience. When witness came back to school from dinner about 20 minutes past one,the prisoner was at the school door waiting for her. She asked her what she had caned her girl for, and she said "What do you think I have caned her for ?" Prisoner then struck her in the breast and knocked her in the cloak room. Witness said she could not stand this and would suminona her. Toe prisoner then said if she was to be summoned she would give her something to summon her for. She struck her again in the face and under the jaw. Witness did not remember anything more. Frederick William Spencer, Main-street, Frodsham, stated that about 1.30 p.m. on Tuesday 15'1 March, he was in Church-street near the schools when he saw the prisoner and Miss Roberts. They were having words, and he saw the prisoner lift her hand to strike the com- plainant and they both fell into the cloak room. The complainant said she would summon her and then the prisoner made use of very bad language and struck the complainant in the face and under the jaw. Miss Roberts was dazed by the last blow and witness sent ' for Dr. Selby and he removed her home in a cab. Sergeant Clays stated he met the prisoner and asked her what the disturbance was about. She said she had Riven her a good hiding, and would give her some mere if she caned her child. She also said that she would , swing for her. Mr. Steward ,one of the managers of the school,stated to the Bench that the committee had instructed him to press the case as tly-y wanted it to be known that par- enta could not,and must not, go to the school and in^er- ' fere with the mistress or teachers in any way, but must make any complaint to the committee. Prisoner was committed to prison for three months with hard labour and bound over in the sum of £lO to keep the peace for six months,or go to gaol for an addi- tional three months.

The Chairman warned the public about interference, and said that teachers must be protected.

RITNOORN FOOTBALL NOTES.

Runcorn's glorious career in the Cop C,mpetition has at last been checked. But, it is somewhat a consolation to know that their progress has not been stayed by an inferior, but by the best club in the Lancashire section of the Northern Rugby Union. I( was hardly expected that Runcorn would survive Saturday's encounter al. though a few cherished pleasant hopes that Runcorn might manage to leave the arena as victors of the day. This was not so, as is shown by the result. The meeting was undoubtedly the titbit of the third round and it was not surprising that the rails that separated the field of play from the other part of the ground, were peered. The increased price of admis.ion evidently did not Mter many from witnessing a grand fight.

On the whole I think the committee of th Runcorn Club are perfectly satiified that they have travelled so tar in the competition- The progress of the club during the past few weeks, has undoubtedly relieved the mind of the energetic treasurer, Mr. Charles Peweell, and the cemmittee. The revenue will now rise,—after the two gatos which accrued from the Brighouse matches and the bumpieg attendance at Oldham—to a very coneider- able sum,and will help the ex-champions and the officials connected with them to hold their heads above water for some time to come.

tipPAing generally with regard to the game it may be said that there was a deal to much exchange kicking in the match for it to be interesting to watch. The passing what little there was, was splendid, particularly on the Oldham side. The homesters were clever and very speedy. The Cestrians were a shade slow, and lacked finish. Houghton was entirely off form and he did not get rid of the oval with his usual rapidly. But- terworth was undoubtedly the shining light on the visi- tors side, whilst Faulkner and Jolley worked exceedingly hard behind the pack. Langley, Moores, Farmer, and Clayter, were the pick of the forwards.

kt the usual weekly meeting of the officials of the Northern Union held on Monday night at Manchester, Lightfoot, of Runcorn, was su,pended for a month for misconduct against Oldham on Saturday. Brooke of Brighouse Rangers was suspended sine die and Sugden, of the same club, for the rest of the season, for offence g arising out of the match against Runcorn a fortnight ago.

LOCAL INQUEBY.-A. BREAKDOWN.

On Thursday afternoon Messrs. John Thompson, Charles Henry Booth, and foomas Boxier, a committee appointed by the Chtshire County Council, held an in- quiry at the Court-house, Frodshans, with reference to the application of the Frodshain PAriti Council for the formation of an urban district council for the parish ot Frodsbam. The following app,‘are‘a to oppose the' ap. plication:—Mr. John Ashton (clerk to the Runcorn Rural 1 District Council),Messrs. J Moseley Williams,T. Wright. T. Lewis, S. Woodward (members of the Rural Counail); Mr. Wallis (representing the Manchester Ship Canal Co), Mr. C. E. Linaker (representing the overseers of Prod- sham and certain property owners and ratepayers in the district), Mr. Philip SpeAtunso, and others. Mr. Reginald Potts, clerk to the County Council, read the memorial of the Parish Council, which set forth that the population of Frodsharn was upwards Of 3,300, and was thereby constituted a populous district. The rate- able value was £13,316 101., being considerably more than some township already possessed Of Urban District Councils. The resolutdm to meincrializ.3 the County Council on the subject was carried by seven votes to five. The Chairman (Mr. John Th nnpson) thou invited evidence in support of the application. Mr. J. Edwards said that hi was one who was to favour of an urban council. He had nothing to say, because.he had cease to the conclusion that the decision the com- mittee would arrive at was a foregone conclusion, and 1610 re than that, with all due deference to the Chairman, Le thought the township of Frodshain, or rather the ratepayers of Frodsham,had been treated with very scant courtesy by the committee of inquiry. This was a work- ingmen's question, and a tradesmen's question. If they wanted a political meeting they would call it for seven or eight o'clock in the evening, but that meeting had been called at a quarter to three in the afternoon, when workingmen could not appear in support of a memorial which meant bread for them. He took the preliminary objection that it was an improper hour to hold the inquiry. The Chairman : I suppose, Mr. Edwards, you would be able to voice the opin- ion of the workingmen who are not present to-day.— Mr. Edwards also took objection to Mr. Baxter sitting on the committee as he was opposed to the application. —The Chairman said the electors of the district had re- turned Mr. Baxter to the County Council and it was usual to put one local member on the committee.--No evidence was, however, given in support of the appliea. than. Some witnesses having been heard in opposition, the inquiry was closed.

NOT ES

The " I{,vtio fl-ue' contain ,flese curious fact as to the influence ot the Dreyfus aff Lir upon M. Zola's own y-dume. When the book appeared sixty-eight thonsan,i copies had been ordered ; but ,hiring the trial snmo teo thousand orders were countermanded in France. Th- interest in the book grew in other countries, firceen thousand copies mote big shipped than was meow. In general, when M. ZJIa publishes a novel, one-filth of the edition remains in Paris, one-filth is taken up by tbe railway booksellers,one-fifth only is sent to ;be coun- try, while two-fifths are ordered by foreign countries. Russia is the greatest buyer of French fiction, Gfllnany comes next, then England, and then Italy.

The South Shields licensing Cases, which raised the quastion of whether magistrates may receive sums of money for public purposes in consideration of new licences being granted, were concluded on Saturday in a Divisional Curt. It was stated that in one of the cases the applicant had offered as much as £l,OOO. The magistrates justified their acceptance of this money by stating that there had beon a great increase in public- house values, and that they wished to secure a portion of the unearned increment for the benefit of the public. Mr. Asquith, who appeared in opposition to the magis- terial arrangement, said it was admitted to be bad in law, but it was contended that there was no remedy. He maintained that there was a remedy. Mr. Justice Wills, in giving judgement, eaid the magistrates had no more right to exact £l,OOO for public purposes than for their own pockets. If such conditions could be attached to the grant of new licences it would not be a couple of years before there was a kind of auction and licences were sold to the highest bidders. The arrangement was open to every description of abuse. Mr. Justice Darling said many people might think it desirable to impose such conditions as the :South Shields magistrates had laid down, but it could only done by Parliament. A mandamus directing the magistrates to hear and deter- mine the cases according to law was granted.

A recent paragraph in a consular report stated that a liquor having the character of port wine had been nada from barley in Germany. We now learn from the "British and Colonial Druggist" that a large establish- ment is at work at Wandsbek,near Hamburg, producing tnese so-called " Malten-Weine," which are said to have the characteristics of the wines of Southern Europe, owing to the entirely new process of fermentation to which they are subjected. Pasteur is credited with the original discovery of the process, but he never carried it bcyond the theoretical point,and it was left to a Danish savant, Mr. Hansen, and a German bacteriologist, Dr. Sauer,to reduce theory into practice.

It has been finally settled that the Emperor William's pilgrimage to Jerusalem is to take place early next November. On his return journey,his Majesty will visit Yildiz Kiosk,whore the Sultan is already making prepar- ations for his reception. The last time that the German Empsror paid a visit to Constantinople the wonderful Byzantine mosaics in the Kakarieh Djami were un- covered, after having bean hidden for over three cen- turies,and everything well done to produce as favoura- ble an impression on the Emperor as possible, even the streets through which his Imperial Majesty passed being made level with a thick layer of sand to conceal the general want of paving.

A curious effect of hard cyrling is reported from France. Out of the last batch of conscripts, no fewer than eight well known cyclists, some of them enjoying European renown,were rejected as physically incapable of doing duty in the ranks. Hypertrophy and other diseases of the heart were the chief causes of these , rejections, which occasioned profound astonishm,nt tO the candidates thernsdves.One would have thought th t such wrecks of athletic humanity might at all events have been utilised as military cyclists, wno are not unknown in the French army.

A suppssedly microbe-Proof dwelling hss been built at Yokohama, Japan, by Dr. Tan der Hayden. The walls of this building are plates of glass set in metal fasten- ings and made air-tight. lisar the roof there is a email opening for the outflow of air from the living rooms, so arranged that no air can enter that way. Air from out- side can get in only through a tube, whose opening is at some!distance from the house. The air that enters is filtered first through cotton-batting, and then is sterd- -1 led by passing through glycerine.

FIRE AT MESSRS. CROSFIELDS' LrYSRPOOL WORKS. A fire, which at first threatened grave. conse- quences, occurred on Saturday afternoon in the soapworks of Mesirs. Joseph Crosqeld and Sons, Limited,Blundell-st.and liitchen-st.,Liverpool. The Premises in question are of an extensive character, and have been erected a very long time, and run through from Blundell-et. into Kitchen-street two parallel thoroughfares at right angles to Jamaicast. E hortly before three o'clock on Saturday after- noon Inspector Grant and Sergeant Clucas ob- served smoke issuing from. the roof of the work, and immediately afterwards discovered that the place was on fire. The Central Fire Brigade from Hatton-garden were summoned and were quickly on the scene with hose carriage and the steamer Liornby, under Chief Superintendent Willis and Superinteudei,t Thomas. The outbreak, i it seems, originated n the oilroom of the works, and it did not take more than two or three minutes before the safety of the gigantic block of buildings, with their inflammable contents, was in imminent jeopardy. An abundant supply of water was ob- tainable, and the brigade threw themselves into the work of subduing the conflagration with great courage and smartness. From the internal arrange- ment of the structure the task of the firemen was a hazardous as well as an extremely difficult one. Being, of course, unacquanited , with the rooms, some of the firemen whilst plying the hose in the thick suffocating , smoke narrowly escaped falling into the tanks, whilst others fell full length on the floors. By dint of close plucky battling for about an. hour, the bri- gade had the satisfaction of extinguishing the flame,and averting what at the beginning appeared to be an outbreak fraught with serious results as far as injury to property was concerned. Alto- gether there were upwards of seventy firemex en- gaged, with a great number of branches from the main, beside the steamer Itornby. The Salvage Corps were abo present.

SERIOCS CHARGE AGAINST Two YOI7THS. ALLEGED ATTEMPTED OFFICE BREAKING. On Friday morning at the Warrington Police Court, Matthias Pye and Peter Dutton,youths of about 17,were charged with being found on the enclosed premises of Messrs. Pochin and C ..,Bank Quay,with intent to com- mit a felony, on Thursday night.—The Chief Constable said that the prisoners were originally charged with attempting to break and enter the office of Messrs. Pochin and Co.,chemical manufacturers, Bank Quay,but he bad altered the charge in order that they might he dealt with here instead of at Liverpool. About 8,30 oa Thursday evening Martin Glynn, fireman in the employ of Messrs. Pochin and Co., had occasion to go to the entrance gate with a lightel lamp. When near the office he found a large crowbar lying on the ground near the dice door. Near by he also found a large sledge hammer, and on examining the office door lie found a determined attempt had been made to break it open. He gave information to the police and Detectives Barlow and Roper, in the couee of enquiries, found that Pye had been discharged from the Chemical vac rks, and went to his lodgings, 87, Cartwright-street. There he made several conflicting statements. The Minshulls, who lived at the house, identified the hammer as their coal- hammer, Pye WHY brought to the Police Station and stated "It was me and Peter Mitton who did it and no- body else. They only gave me 10i, instead of 14s. and lithe man had not come with the lamp I should have been into that office in another minute." Dutton was also apprehended and said that Pye told him they had not given hint enough money.—Martin Glyno, fireman, and Detective Bartow corroborated.—The Chief Con- stable said that Pye had a had record and had been com- mitted of theft before.—The magistrates sent Pye to gaol for 2 months with hard labour, and Dutton for 14 days' hard labour. Tan TRAVELLING Cowl:mos AND Tint LANDLADY'S CLOCK. At the Warrington Borough Police Court, on Monday, before Mr. T. Birchall (in the chair), and other maeis- trees. William Stocks, a travelling conjuror, hailing from Cheater, was charged with stealing, by means of a trick, a clock, value Is. 81.. the property of Margaret Hinds, Percives-fold.—Prisoner pleaded guilty.—The Chief Constat le stated that last week the prisoner took lodgings at Mrs. Hinds' lodging-house in Percival'a-fold. He was hard up on Friday evening, and on seeing a clock which was not going he said to Mrs. Hind. "That clock has been over-oiled." Be removed a little of the ail from the works and replaced it on the wall. Be charged Mrs. Hinds et for the process, but the clock still refused to go. He then said the clock would never go until it had a new spring. Mrs. Hinds said he might take it out on Saturday morning and have a new spring put in it. Instead of doing an he went from one public house to another trying to ell it. A man eventually bought it for 2s. at the Sceptre Inn. Information was given to Mrs. Hinds, and the prisoner was apprehended In the evening by P.C. Clarke.—After evidence had been given in support of the Chief Constable's statement, the magiatrates committed the prisoner to gaol for one month,

CAIRO-STREET AMATETT Ft DRAMATIC SOCIETY.

On the evenings of Monday and Tuesday the Amateur Dramatic Society in connection with Cairo-st. Church gave performances in aid of the Warrington and District Nursing Association. There was a large audience on etch occasion. The programme consisted of two come- diettas : " Cut off with a Shilling," and " Withered Leaves," the first of which was entirely humorous, while the second included a little sentiment. " Cat off with a Shilling" almost tells one its character with its title. There is the disinherited nephew with a pretty but not wealthy wife, and the irate uncle. A reconciliation is eventually effected by the winsome artfulness of the wife. The parts were taken as follows :—Sam Gay- thorne. Mr. 11. S. Knowles ; Colonel Berners, Mr. J. S. Hill ; Kitty Gaythnrne, Miss Lilian Allen. A big pro. portion of the acting fell upon the two latter, both of whom interpreted their parts with ease and naturalness. ' Mr. Knowles made a good Sans Gaythorne though he was scarcely so free from nervousness as his brother artistes. " Withered Leaves" is a much more pre- tentious piece and contains five characters, viz. : Sir Conyers Conyere, Mr. J. Si. Hill; Torn Conyers. Mr. 1 Gerrard; Arthur Middleton,Mr C. H. Wood ; Cecil Vane, Mr. T. C. Hollingsworth ; tidy Conyers, Miss Agnes Knowles; May Rivera, Miss Florence Potter. The piece is of a more complicated nature than its predecessor. Arthur Middleton and Tom Conyers are bosmat friends, the former of whom a year ago was a suitor for the hand of !Wis.! May Rivers, At the present time Me•Ceeit Vane is in the position of an accepted suitor, when Mid- dleton arrives on the scene. Vans is known to his rival as an arrant scamp an fortune-hunter, and some high words between them are heard by Miss Rivers. She spurns the fickle Vane from her and returns to her old love Arthur Middleton. In the meantime the three Con- yera' are working* out a little play of their own. Pre- vious to his father's second marriage, Torn Conyers has left home after a " row," and subsecpuntly turns up at his father's honoymnon resort. Here Lady Conyers di,- eeVery who he is, and after ROMA amusing adventures linally reconciles her husband with his son, and all " live happy ever after," With ,few, if any ex- ceptions, all the artistes seemed' quite at home in their parts and gave a very successful performance. Messrs. Hill, Wood and Gerrard were excellent, while nothing but high praise can be accorded Miss Knowles and Miss P )tter. All had an equal share of stage play including Mr. Hollingsworth, lin had it must be said, apparently a slight tendency to the melodramatic. Taken as a whole the Society has enchanced Its reputa- tion by the performance, which was in every respect the equal if not the superior of the Olie 'which give sock satisfaction twelve months ago. A strong orchestra, under the conductorship of Mr. G. J. Allen rendered the following programme of music in a very creditable manner. Overture " Masaniello " '(Auber) ; waltz, " •morettentenzs" (S. Gung'i); overture. "Marionetten" (C. Garnet) ; dance, "Cz 'int*. No. 6 " (G. Nichiels) ; waltz, " Nachtachwarmer " (R. Fels) ; march, " Guard of Honour" (Karl Kaps).

THE CHESHIRE MEN DEFEATED.

This important fixture was decided at Water Shed. dings, Oldham, on Saturday, before 12,000 spectators. Both teams were at their otrengrat and punctually to time Frater set the oval rolling for Oldham. The open- ing movements were rather slow, and for fully five min- utes kicks and countel-kicks were freely indulged in. Uradually Oldham got d ,wn,and the first item of prom- inence was a fine run by Williams. Still keeping up the pressure the ball was passed to Taylor,who punted over the Runcorn line,and Houghton Al!.)mirv.; the ball to roll touched down,but, unfortunately,lost possession,and A. Lees dashing up fell On the oval ant gained the referee's verdict. This was undoubtedly a hard piece of luck for Runcorn, as there was not the slightest doubt that Houghton touched the ball down. Thorals took the place, but his kick went anywhere but near the posts. Runcorn alter this played up str.mgly, and the ball was taken to the Oldham half,where It was kicked to Martin. Lightfoot, rushing up, turned his back on his opponent when he was about to kick, for wbich trivial offence he was ordered off the ground. Thus it was seen early on that luck was dead against the Cestrians. Resuming with fourteen men Runcorn put forth new energy, and they were quickly swarming round the Oldham posts, Butterworth in particular making strenuous eff uts to break through. However, mum trouble was soon to follenr. Gayter and Plater Gal- tided, witk the result that both were terribly cut, and Gayter having to leave the field left Runcorn with thir- teen men. In no way daunts I the visitors continued to have much the best of the play. An effort by Butterworth was oherb, and he was only held up on the line. Three times in einiak succession did the same player drop at goal, but tke ball in each case went just outside. Houghton made a grand attempt from a mark Iby Moores. At this stag* Gayter pluckily returned, his head beiog literally covered with bandages. Right up to the interval Oldham were defending. The score then stood—Oldham, one try (three points) : Runcorn, nil. Farmer commenced the s.ccori moiety, and Oldham were soon on the aggressive. After a lot of exchanges and kicking, the passing of the home backs got to work. The ball was sent across te Martin, who failed so gather it, but deliberately struck it with his hand and aunt it over the line. Lawton, razing up, secured, and was awerded try. Considering the extraordinary ability which was displayed by the referee, it seemed rather peculiar that this point, which was obsained by glaring infringement, should have been allowed, and it only added to the cup of Runcorn's bitterness. A. few minutes later a passing movement by the Runcorn backs was interrupted by Hurst, and the Oldham flyer mad. tracksfor the Runcorn line, but only to gain the au- quaintance of ['ought n and have an opportunity of embracing mother earth in a rather unceremonious manner. Some pretty exchanges passing between Faulkner, Butterworth, and Warder made things look omninous for the home team, and Warder was only stopped by being pushed into touch a few yards from the line. A moment later West was given an easy chance after some good passing, but he failed to gather the ball, and thus a golden opportunity was lost. Vali was evidently the expiring effort of Runcorn, as following some passing by the home backs Hurst made a fine dash down the touch line,and succeeded in ground- ing at the corner flag. But still the goal-kicking was off. Just before time Oldham wera penalizld, and Efoughton taking the kick sent the ball to Hurst, who dashed into the centre of the field from the touch line, and coolly taking a dropped kick sent the hall sailing over the bar, the game ending in a win for Oldham by one goal three tries (11 points) to Runcorn nothing. The following did duty for Runcorn :—S. Houghton, back ; T. Warder, J. Butter orth, G. Robinson, and B. West, threequarters ; J. Faulkner and J. Jolley, half backs ; S. Walker, .1. Langley, G. THE WAARINGICjii G.,IS WORKS QUESTLON.

The "Ga. World" says :—Mr. Councillor Roberts, of Warrington, is mu3h exercised over the small amount contributed by the gas works in aid of rates, and, with the Chairman of the Gas Committee as seconder, he has got the Town Council to order a return for the period 1890.7 showing the coat of all renewal+ and repairs ren- dered necessary by ordinary wear and tear, of all re- newals rendered necessary by other circumstances, and of all additions to the plant. Of course, Mr. Roberts' object is te ascertain whether say charges have been placed to revenue that ought to have gone to capital. Though we have a suspicion that Mr. Roberts bas been led to ask for this return through looking at one side of the accounts only, we are in perfect sympathy with him. In examining the accounts since 1889 to the end of last year, Mr. Roberts found that while only £4,000 had been contributed in Aid of rates, £50,000 had been spent in "extra gas manufacture and in statutory charges." But where the connection between the two figures comes in we fail to see. On the contrary, the latter sure suggests that the suspicion that capital has been favoured at the expense of reveriue is unfounded. But however the facts stand it is only right that they should be known,and if it be found that revenue has been unduly saddled in past years, Mr. R )b-rts will, no doubt, see to it that the practice in the tuture will be different. Parliament com- pels present day gas consumers to free the gasworks from debt for the benvfit of future generations of con- sumers or ratepayers, and while they are boa ring that load it is not fair to put upon them the additional one of building new gasworks. What railiaraent says is that you must free the gas-works of to-day from debt by a certain date; it does not say that you must build an- other gasworks to meet the larger dvmand of that future date.

WARRINGTON PHOTOGRAPHIC SOCIETY.

EXHIBITION AND CONCERT.

I The Warrington Photographic Society has bean an un- assuining but quirriy active little body for a few years. Its doings have hi, herto been mostly confined to the knowledge of its inernhers hut at last the Society has cone before the tihic with a concert and exhibition of slidea,etc. The debut was made in Parr Hall on Saturday evening, end chit it was a success of a most befittiog character, no one, we think, will be prepared to deny. A strong feature of the programme provided was its variety, and this eornbined with its general excellence made it a source of unalloyed pleasure to the very large audience. The artistes were Mrs. D.S. Stone (soprano), Mr. Peter I. Bate (nags), Mr. R. H. Pearson (violinist), an I Mr. W. H. Payton (accompanist). Both the vocal- ists achieved a most gratifying success. Mrs. Stone (the wife of the hard-working secretary of the Society) gave very tasteful interpretation, of " The Dar Homeland " and " When you Forget." Her voice, though not of the variety commonly called robust or strong, still possessed enough volume to make it dis- tinct and clear even to the back of tbe large hall. Mrs. Stone's first effort was much the best, the song itself being better ruiteri to her expressive vocalisation. The encore ,lemancled was of an enthusiastic character. Mr. Rate was decidedly in good voice, and rolled forth "Oh, oh. hear the will winds blow," and "Foe Nude-4,11Ra,- a vigour and tenefulness which won him hearty aPp'aiise. In response to the demands of the audience foil 'wing upon his rendering of the last named song,Mr. Bate gave the well-worn favourite" len'her O'Flynn." Warrington reanple are so accustomed to hearing and applauding Mr. P :arson in his capacity :IS violinist that his success in..Carzona,'"Mobile Perpertio,"and "Faust" was in the ordinary course of events. Fie was con- strained to repeat "Mobile Perpe'uo." Mr. Payton accom- panied in a maneer which considerably enhanced all the musical items The cinematograph, being still in a great measure a novelty, was perhaps, the premier attraction. Mr. J. T. Chapman, of Manchester, went through four series of films, the subject matter .4 which was produc- tive of much enjoyment and amusement. Some of the more popular pictures were the Haunted Chamber, the Death of Nelson, Saowballing, and the scenes in which bre ikiue WAVPA were shown. Nothing more realistic could be irnagieed. At inter:els also views taken by the following members of the society were thrown upon the 4creen :—Messrs. J. Fairhurst, H. N. Houghton,J. Arm- strong, H. Bind, G. Kirby, J. Dolan, T. Welsby, F. W. Knowles, G. Orton, W. W. Winstanley and Mrs. Smithson. Is addition to local views there were pourtrayerl scenes from almost every part of England, and very beautiful most .if ahem were. That the society has within he ranks ,runia more thin oi-dinar', capable amateurs was at once demonstrated by the quality of the exhibited slils. Meesr.4.Armatrong,lioughton,Bo.nd, and Kirby had all got a colle:tion of views containing stone exquisite pictures, thoina of the last named gentle-- man being largely obtained during it tour in Norway. It may be interesting to our readers to know that the Society was f ninded in 1887 and during the last few years the meetings have been held in the Committee Room, or toe Lecture Room, of the Warrington Museum, though these have been found somewhat inconvenient, owing to the lack of accominndation for dark room pur- poses and fer carrying on the practical work of the Saciaty. Some time ago the Society took in hand the photeeraphinv of old buildings, &c., in this district for the Warrington Museum and with the aid of Mr. Made- ley, the curator of the Museum, a list was prepared and several places were visited and photographed, but the difficulty of making the necessary enlargements and the lack of dark room accommodation was a drawback to the work being proceeded with. TtleAtl difficulties have now been somewhat overcome, the Society having been allowed by the Ti.clinied Committee the use of two rooms in the School el Science building in Wilson Pat- ten-st.,one of which has been fitted up As A photographic ,lark room, with an enlariinei lantern, and the other is used fee the meetings and for the library. It was with the object of raisiog funds to defray the cost of this work, which has henn considerable, that the exhibition and concert were held.

THE ACCIDENT AT LATCHFORD LOCKS.

LEGAL PROCEEDINGS IN LONDON.

On Tuesday, in the Admiralty Division of the High Court, Mr. Justice Barnes had before him the motion of the plaintiffs in the case of the Manchester Ship Canal Company v. the Owners of the s.s. Harold, in objection to a report of the assistant registrar. The matter arose out of an action brought by the plaintiffs against the owners of the s.s. Harold, of Glasgow, to recover dam- ages for injuries done to the Latchford Lock of the canal. It appeared that on the 10th April, 1895, the Harold,a vessel of 1,000 tons register, was proceed- ing down that part of the canal which lies between the upper lock and the intermediate lock (Latch- ford) at full speed. The gates of the interrnsdi- ate lock were opened for her reception, hut in ' passing through the Harold struck against the " leaves," carried them away,end seriously damaged the sate. The Immediate result of the accident was that the " leaves " were carried down on to the lower gates and prevented their being shut. The upper gates were slammed to, hoerever,and this prevented the water rushing down the canal. A telegram was sent to Liverpool for assistance —first to the Mersey Docks and Harbour Board and next to the Liverpool Salvage Associatiou,and they sent their powerful steamer Ranger. She at once set to work,and succeeded in pulling the leaves free of the lower gates, which were then shut. The Ranger was next engaged ' to tow the intermediate gates up the canal to have them repaired, and for this purpose greenhart wood had to be procured at considerable trouble. The gates were ultimately repaired, and put in position. The defendants admitted their liability, and at the reference the plaintiff company, amongst items, put in a claim of £3,118 3t. 81. in respect of losses sustained by having to take tho intermediate gates up the canal ; b t the assistant registrar only allowed £2,157. Ile also reduced a claim of the plaintiffs from £3,470 to £1,735 made in respect of some new steel gates which had to be used while the other gates were being repaired. The assistant registrar held that the defendants ought not to pay snore than half the coat of the steel gates, on the ground that they would be of use to the plaintiffs on some future occasion. The plain- tiffs further complained that the assistant registrar im- properly disallowed a claim of £BO for the interruption of the canal,that be did not allow sufficient interest,and that he made a wrong order as to costs. There was a aross-motion by the defendants as to certain other small itents allowei by the registrar. Mr.L E. Pyke,Q.C.,and Mr.B.Aspinall appeared for the defendant appellants. At the conclusion of the arguments, his lordship intimated he would reserve his judgment.

Police-constable Wiflian Hinsdale, of the Bolton force, who is already under remand on a charge of shop-break- ing,was remonded at Wigan on Wednesday on a further charge of the same kind. A sensation has been created by the discovery of 159 keys at his residence. Findlater, the renowned piper of Dargai, was among the invalids for Netley Hospital brought to Southampton on Monday on the troopship Simla.

MR. PERCY DAVIES AND THE SOUTHPORT G OLF CLUB. _ _

Mr. Percy Davies, of Warrington, having offered a handsome silver shield to be played for by teams representing the resident memuers of the clubs, the first, competition took place on the links of the Southport Club, at Churchtown on Saturday, in fine weather. The competitions are by match play, and the club which WRIS two out of three matches will become the absolute owner .31 the shield. At Saturday's meeting Soutuport wodeasily, having a majority of 29 holes. The Southport players won eight games, lost one, and halved one. The second match will take place on the Birkdals links in September.

WARRINGTON COLLEGE.

DARESBURY SCHOOL BOARD. THE PLAYGROUND QUESTION. A special meeting of the Daresbray School Board was held on Thursday last week, in the schoolroom, Dares- bury. The Rev. F. T. Clarke presided, and there were also present Mr. Tickle, (vice-chairman), Captain Rogder, Mr. Brooms, Mr. Cooper, Mr. Phillips, and Mr. Sharpe (deputy-clerk). The busine4s of the meeting was to hear a report from the deputation which had waited upon Mr. John White (representing Sir Gilbert Greenall) with reference to the playground near the schools.—Mr. Broome asked who appointed the deputation.—Mr. Cooper replied that Mr. Phillips had proposed the vies -chairman and himself, and Miss Dutton had seconded it and added the name of Mr. Phillips to it.— Mr. Broome : It was totally out of order.—Captain Rodger : They ought to have given notice of that resolu- tion before adopting it.—Mr. Cooper: We were quite in order—Captain Rodger : I am perfectly certain that you were not in order.—The Chairman: It was out of order altogether to appoint a deputation in that manner.—Captain Rodger and Mr Broome went on to say that the deputation appointed itself.—Mr Cooper : I don't agree with that as the deputation was perfectly in order.—After some further discussion Captain Rodger , hought they might let the matter drop and go on with the business.—The report of the deputation was then proceeded with, and it included a letter from Mr John White dated March 11th andlwhich stated that the dept.'. tation waited upon him consisting of Messrs. Tickle, Cooper,and Phillips with reference to the acquisition of the land. Sir Gilbert Greenall was still unwilling to autherise the sale of the land for the purpose required but he had no power to lease it for longer than 21 years except for building purposes. There appeared to be a difficulty for the Beard to get money on security foi so short a lease. He was however authorised by Sir Gilbert to say that he himself would advance all the money required, which must not exceed £l5O, and charge the Board 2i per cent on the outlay with £1 nominal rent for the land. This would amount to £4 15s.per annum only—an additional one-eighth of a penny on the rates. This would be far less than if they had harrowed he money elsewh(re.The deputation received this proposal very favourably, and he trusted that it would commend itself to the other members of the Board, and that they would not, as on the occasion of the last offer—which most people con.idered a gener- ous one—draw forth unjustifiable, not to say abusive, remarks, which only tended to mislead the public, and were eminently calculated to delay the carrying out of a very urgent and advisable improvement.—Captain Rodger cmtended that the remarks whic`i had been made concerning the last offer wsre fully justified under the circumstances. It was a very unfortunate thing that it was necessary to make such remarks. He, how- ever, consider, d this offer a generous one, and he was quite prepared to acknowledge it as such.—Mr, Bronme said they could not accept the offer until the next meeting as they would have to rescued the resolution to acquire the land by compulsory purchase—Alter a little further discussiou Mr. Cooper gave notice that at the next meeting he would move the rescinding of this resolution for acquiring the laud by compulsory pur- chase,amd ac eptance of the present offer.—The meeting shortly afterwards terminated.

OPEN CuMPETITION_ AT THE

ay MNASIIJ3I.

There was only a moderate attendance at the first annual gymnastic competition held in the Borough Gymnasium, Palmyra Square,Warrington, on Saturday evening. Mr. J. Kinsley, the director of exercises at the institution was unable to be present through serious iliness,and the duties which would naturally fall upon him were satisfactorily discharged by Mr. G. N. Vernon,the energetic hon. secretary. Mr. D. Jones presided,and amongst those also present were Councillors Minion and Evans (members of the Gymnasium Committee). Consi- dering that this was the first competition of the I kind at Warrington, the number of entries was , very good, including as they did, the names of some of the leading gymnasts in Lancashire and Cheshire. The proceedings commenced with a dumb-bell exercise by members of the War- rington gymnasium, the pianoforte accompani- ments:being played by Miss Clayton. In the com- petition, the competitors who numbered ten, went through one voluntary exercise upon. the parallel bars and horizontal bar, the maximum points awarded on each apparatus being 20. Marks were awarded for the best exhibition of strength, skill, agility and grace. The concluding item in the I competition was rope climbing, the competitor being obliged to climb the rope hand over hand without using the feet, both legs to be on one side of the rope. One mark was awarded for each yard climbed the first time up the rope, and two marks for each yard after. The competitors were :—J. Jackson, Folds-road Gymnasium, Bolton; J. Johnson, Y.M.C.A. Gym- nasium,Manchester;T.Butt erworth,Lads' Club Gym- nasium, Stockport; A.Lund,Social Club Gymnasium, Manchester; W. J. Burrows, Balfour Gymnasium, Liverpool ; H. Bradley, Y.M.C.A. Gymnasium, Man- cheeter ; H. Preston, Proctor Gymnasium, Hulme Manchester; G.T. Mellor,Y.M.C.A. Gymnasium,Man chester; A. Mottram, Warrington Gymnasium; and H.Jones, Simpson Memorial Gymnasium, Moston. It will be seen that there was only one Warring- ton competitor—A. Mottram—and he just failed to receive the third prize by losing points which it was anticipated he would get in rope climbing. In the horizontal bar exercise, T. Butterworth secured 19 points, his rearest rival being Jackson with 18 points. In the parellel bars, Butterworth was beaten by one competitor—Johnson—who gained 19 points to Butterworth's 17, but still the latter was able to hold the best aggre- gate on the two exercises. In rope-climbing, Burrows was the best with 25 marks, Butterworth coming next with 23. The total scores are appended from which it will be seen that Butterworth won the first prize rather easily with 59 points to his credit. The second prize fell to Mellor with 51i marks, and the third was captured by Bradley. The latter was run very close by Mottram, and Burrows who each made 47 points. Scores:— COMPETITORS. Horizontal Parallel Rope. Totals. Bar. Bars. - - THE WORN-OUT POSTMAN. Sir,—l noticed the parr.gripm (March 4) on the retire- ment of Alexander Flaher fr,an ill-health. Feeling in- terested in the matter I made enqairies and find that during the 22 years he has been in the Service,hia round, includlng Collins Green,has been in all weathers from 18 to 20 miles a day—roughly speaking 160,000 milss—a protracted pilgrimage. TEIE ASSESSMENT OF PUBLIC-HOUSES IN

WARRINGTON.

Sir,—Would you kindly insert the enclosed letter addressed by our Firm to the Chairman of the Finance Committee. If one public-house is assessed at one half its value, as compared with another public-house, it follows that public-houses, similarly assessed must be at half value, as compared with other property. Either certain public-houses, are half assessed or other property le doubly assessed. Which is it ?—Yours faithfully, W. A. ISIS/101" AND CO., LTD.

.1. Shaw Green, Esq., LP., Chairman of the Finance Committee, Warrington C:iporeion, Warrington. Dear Sir,—Why is the Bath's Hotel, which cost the Warrington Corporation £3,100, assessed at £ll9 rate- able, whilst the Seven Stars property. which cost double the amount, is only assessed at £l2O ? How it that the Queen's flo•elowned by the Warrington Corporation,is assessed at £llO rateable, whilst the Feather's Hotel for which £lB,OOO :s asked, is assessed at £62 rateable ? Should not the assessment of public-houses in Warring- ton be comparatively equal ? Can yen tell us why the overseers in Manchester, Accrington, Crewe and other places are looking into the matter ? Yours faithfully, W. A. BISHOP AND CO., LTD.

STOCKTON HEATH'S PUBLIC 14E1'71

Sir,—l have watched with interest the proceedings of Stockton Heath Parish Council during the past twelve months, and I was naturally curious to know the result of the voting at the annual meeting of parishioners. Messrs. England and Mason, who championed the cause of the public against private interests are at the bottom of the lot. . One is apt to indulge in cynical musings as ta the influences at work in popular elections, and endeavour to follow up the causes which result in one man being elected, and another—sometimes a better man—being rejected. But I f,rbsar. What I wish to do is to call attentim to the Nct that Messrs. England and Maxon who incurred considerable obloquy of a sort for disinterested service are, strange to say, not ..lected. Surely the qualities of intelligence, and independence should command our approval and admiration, and I am not without hope that at the poll which is to take place, the ratepayers gen rally, will not endorse the voice of the annual meeting, but will include Masers. England and Mason among the successful candidates.— Yours, &c.

SPECT &TOR.

THE RECENT DEBATE ON SPIRITUALISM. Sir,—Having read in your issue of the 12th the ietter by Mr. H.Whitham in reference to the recent debates on Spiritualism and also in the same issue your report of the excellent sermon by the Rev. J.Yonge, may I be per- mitted to state, that Secularism can never satisfy the hopes and aspirations of mankind, for, as Mr. Yong, so admirably pointed out, "The different religious denom- inations are all evidences of our deep consciousness that this present passing world has clear and close relation- ship with a world that is lasting and eternal." The quotation I have made is so very good that your readers will not mind reading it again, and if it be true iii. the most important thing for mankind to learn and realiz3, for although those who disregard it may consider them- selves too wiae and practical to be taken in by vulgar errors and delusions, yet are they not far from being practically wise ? Correct ideas are as necessary for our moral welfare as healthy food is for our physical well tieing. Is it consistent for a man to encourage and cul- tivate in himself a regard for justice if he does not be- lieve it to be a principle of or to exist in the universe. Oh love, oh, beauty, health and life. Is death triumphant in the strife ? Have ye no hidden power to live Beyond the gloom his shadows give? Is it In vain we live and fight. In vain we struggle for the sight, Shall hope and Lave sad friendship be But withered leaves upon the tree?

Nearly all religious errors contain an important truth. Take the first to which lir. Whithatn refers—predes- tination. Is it not a fact that before the birth of matt nature had evolved ordered predestinated laws of his physical and moral nature ? There is a great tendency to misapply an important truth when it is first forcibly realized. What is the meaning of everlasting punish- ment ? Not that the sinner will be eternally punished, but that it is a law of nature that violations of the har- mony and beauty of the universe are punished now and will be forever mare. How very thankful ought we to be that suffering must follow sin, for it is the only way to separate us from that baleful throng—Yours truly, S. KENYON. Warrington.

WARRINGTON v. RUNCORN. Sir,—When are the Warringtan Fmtball Club officials going in for a better three-quarter line ? Last Saturday's match with Runcorn was only a further proof of the poorness of the home back division. With the exception of Isherwood, I think they wore clearly out shone by their opponents, particularly in the latter stages of the game, for when Runcorn began their passing, the game was rapidly taken towards the home goal, whereas when Warrington began to pass (which was seldom), the ball simply travelled across the field without any apparent gain whatever, and very ol;en being mulled and opportunities lost. This clearly shows a want of practice with the ball, and a want of proper combination. Moores would play considerably better,in my opinion, if lie could keep his temper a little more under control. Our half backs made plenty of openings, but after we have said that, we have said all. No one can deny that we have a good set of forwards. and I am sure it must be very disheartening indeed to them at times when their efforts are so poorly seconded by the hacks. Now,to my way of thinking, there seems to be a "touch" of selfishness in their play, and this is more plaialy seen when there is a new man on the team. I should have thought Hull Kingston would have shown them how to play an unselfish game, but lam disappointed. Now,if this difficulty of "self-play" is to be overcome, our teams must have more social in- tercourse together, such as,l should say,hot-pot suppers, smoking concerts, So., where players and officials can meet together, about once a month. I am sure this could be done on very little expense, and it would be good way of getting the players together oftener and infusing a more "friendly " feeling throughout the club. I happened to be a spectator on Saturday last, and these thoughts struck me as being one way of improving our team. I suppose the other way would be to import better men. Of course good men cannot be got hold of every day, but I should think Warrington should stand as good a chance as any other club, if they chose to speculate a little. Spectators, I think, now-a-days, are not so strong on local talent, as the want of good foot- tall. For instance, take the Widnes-v-Hunslet match a fortnight ago. It was surprising to see the large num. her of Warringtonians on the Widnes ground, and our first team at home too. If our spectators are willing to pay is. railway fare and another 6J. sr is. to see the match, I think it quite proof enough that, if the W.F.C. officials will got good footballers, they will also get big " gates."—Yours truly,

" SPECTATOR."

Mr. Paikin's manager locked up the premises as usual on Tuesday night,and left them secure as ho thought. The following morning, at about nine o'clock, the manager found the place in a state of disorder, but at the time he did not see that anything was missing. He afterwards found that A shirt, silk handkerchief, and muffier were Missing, and also that the catch on the back kitchen widow was broken, evidently wkere the men had effected their entrance. Information was given to the police, and Detectives Barlow and Roper went into the neighbourhood for the purpose of making Moen. vations. At 11.30 p.m. they heard a noise of someone tampering with a window in Mr. Paikin's premises. NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN3

(BY IN OCCASIONAL OORRICSP3NDRN C.)

The meeting in the Wesleyan Chapel on Tuesday in connection with the Lannon Missions was ime- cessFul in more ways than one. Financially the mission will benefit slightly, but it will also have succeeded in enlisting the support and sympathy of many who had hitherto given but little heed to the matter. The case for the mission was very lucidly and pathetically laid before the people by the Rev. W. D. Walters. the general secretary, and by the Rev. Peter Thompson of the East ,London Mission. Some of the stories told by Ow missionaries were very harrowing, and there is not the slightest doubt that the work to be performed by the devoted men and women connected with the mission is of the very highest order.

It is surprising what a very little will attract a crowd of hard-headed British subjects for the sale object of satisfying their curiosity. Last Sunday two special trains conveying some of the baggage and horses belonging to Barnum and Bailey's show

were expected to—pa;s through Earlestown about half-past three in the afternoon. About that time and for some considerable time afterwards the MOSTIOR running parallel with the railway was lined with people, and in Park Road over 100 were gathered ilia group. Neither of the trains appeared however, and many people seemed to think they had beea hoaxed. The trains did actually pass Earlestown, but it was not until after six o'clock on Monday morning, they having occupied about 25 hours on the journey from London.

The school question at Earlestown has reached another stage. It is settled that the proposed school for which we are to be expected to volun- tarily subscribe,is to be a Church of England school, Anyway it appears the condition hedging round the grant of land by Lord Newton is that it must be a C.;norainational (church) school. This is all very well,so far as it goes, but is it fair or reasonable to expect Dissenters to do anything at all towards the erection and support of such a school. When call- ing the meetings and appointing committees, the promoters of the affair very carefully kept back the information that the school would be denomina- tional,but hinted that it might possibly be a British sch.ol, knowing full well,that Lord Newton would not grant the land unless on the understanding that the school would be a church school. The Nonconformists are up in arms against the proposal and quite properly so too,and an energetic crusade against the raiepayers' money being diverted into a channel for the benefit of one c1a..48 only instead of for the whole community might possibly achieve its successful overthrow.

So far as the approaching District Council elec- tions are concerned, very little interest appears to be taken in them,save and except the contest which is inevitable for the representation of Town Hall ward. Contrary to first expectations the fight in this ward has been narrowed down to a dual contest, sir. G.Crossley,who was nominated,having withdrawn. The contest thus rests between Mr. W. Beatty and Mr. Thomas Robinson. The former is a Catholic and the latter a Churchman, but I think this fact will weigh very little in votes of the constituents. Mr. Beatty has a certain claim upon the electors from the fact that he has previously held a seat on the Council and represented the Crow-lane ward with ability. Mr. Robinson po,,.sesees an excellent re- commendation as a district councillor as, belng a practical engineer, his exosrience cannot fail to be of advantage to the Council as a whole. The addresses of both candidates, which have already appeared, seem to agras on one point,that the water supply of the township should be independent and in the hands of the Council. The election takes place on April 4th,and that day I anticipate,will witness one of the stiffest fights which has yet taken place in any ward.

VirESLEYAN LONDON MISSIONS. ---- MEETING AT EARLESIGWN.

Oa Tuesday afternoon and evening special meetings in connection with the London Missions conducted by the Wesleyan Methodist body,were held in the Wesleyan Chapel, Earlestown. A sermon was preached in the afternoon by the Rev. P. Thompson of the East London Mission, and at the close a tea was partaken of by a number of those present. Tne evening mee'ing was presictsd over by the Rev. H. J. Scott. of Southpert,end he was supported by the Rev. W. D. Walters, General Secretary of the London atieeions, Rev. P. Thompson, Rev. F. E. Miller,Rev. P. R. Pasemore, &o. The Chairman made an urgent appeal on behalf of the Missions. They had with them two men who had given their lives to the work, and through them they con- veyed their hearty sympathy with their labours. The Rev. W. D. Walters was much obliged to the chairman for his sympathy with the cause. To give them an idea am to the vast area of London,he would tell them that the population of Lendon was now 8,100,000 people. The area of London was larger than that of New York, Paris,and Berlin put together. It had over 7,000 miles of streets, and, as sueeestive of the moral condition of London, a third of the crime of the whole country was committed in London alone, and if the public houses were placed tneether they would reach over 800 miles. The drink tiaffic there seemed to be on the increase. It was a sad fact that that night in Lon- don, quite 80,000 women would walk the stre-its ready Ito sell body and soul for a morsel of bread, and 10,000 of these poor fallen girls were under 18 years of age, and it was not an uncommon thing,as they had recently 11 found out, for young girls. of 13 veers of age to ply this trade night after night, and a vast majority were those who had come from eountry homes. It was a depl arable fast in connection with their London life that a papula• time twice as large as Manchester, dwelt in single rooms living under conditions which made morality,to say no- thing of religien, almost impossible. They heard a good deal of the wealth of London, but for the most part it was in the hands of men wile had no sympathy with Christ's wark. Only one person in four in the daily population of London earned anything like £1 pie" we-lc so that the vast majority of the people were very poor. It was a suggestive feat that 40,000 men, women and children were every night homelese and friendless, with not a place to shelter their head. He was glad to say that through the Salvation Army and other means, shelter hied been provided for many, but notwithstanding this 8,000 men, women,end childi en slept in the open air. This was a condition of things which was a disgrace to our national life. A quarter of a million single women in Leaden earned less than a shilling per day, and that by working long hours, and 320,000 people in the area of London Merle, were entirely dependent upon casual labour. Every year 110,008 young men from the country who weit to Lon- don to the hospitals, and to learn trades were drawn down into the vortex of came, and he would advice 'those who loved their chiliree to keep , them out of the City life, and not allow them to go unless they were truly converted, and teetotallers,. At the pretent moment thee' had in connection with the London Miseion, quite 1,000 men who had come from the ceuntry, avd who had given their hearts to God, and who were doing splendid Mission work. From inquiries which were made it was found that out if the vast maims of inner Lendon, barely 1± per cent, attended any place of wer- Shin, and yet we called ourselves a Chris- tian country. The Lnidon Mission le its guiding influence had crossed the mighty doen,and every city throeehent Australia had reaped direct results as the inspiration of the London Miesion. The same thing prevailed in New York and Japan. They bed not intended,a few years since.to do what the Len. dot Mission was new directine. They only intended them to have one Mission to one of the meet needed parts of London, they thought that was quite up to the Possibilities of the Methodist Church. Now, instead of hiving one M seion,they had six branches of the London Missi n, with 22 centres, 100 agents, and 14 ministers. They had a splendid body of men in connection with the ion, hut although the men were splendid the women,be thought, were better. He then proceeded tie give a detailed account of the work carried on by the , Wiles connected with the mission which chiefly related to rescue work. He alluded to the " Homes for the dy- I leg," which had recently been instituted, and which were established for the receipt of those homeless people who were discharged from the hospitals as incurablee. The Rev. P. Thompson also delivered an address en somewhat similar lines, and a collection wan made in aid of the funds.

On the first charge the Fudge imposed a sentence of tea days, with the option of a line, and in the other case s sentence of two days impthonment ' without the option of a tine. This descision s(Cims to estathish the principle that those, who sell imitations representing them to be "the same as" Dr. William' Pink Pills are liable under the criminal code. THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, MARCH 25, It9B THE NEW COUNTY COURT AND INLAND REVENUE OFFICES. DESCRIPTION OF TIIE BUILDING.

Considering its size Warrington, as a town, is remarkably deficient in buildings possessing decided claims to architectural beauty. Old-fashioned in many respects, "the good old town" has a great ' deal to pass through ere it may claim an archi- tectural equality with some similar municipalities. Even considering its growth—which is now of a rapid nature—Warrington has received little en- hancement in its outward appearance in the last 50 years. It is true that progress has been made but a townsman can show the visitor little in the archi- tectural line which be may reasonably be proud of. The edifice which forms the subject of our article will, however, be an unquestionably fine addition to the town's external and material charms. It is scarcely an exaggeration to say that the new County Court and Inland Revenue offices in Palmyra Square tor in the handsomest building Warrington is able to boast of. This quarter of the town bids fair to become a very creditable sample of Warring- ton's latest development. Though it may be going over old ground it is not superfluous to state that if ever say public offices needed new headquarters, the local branch of the Inland Revenue Department and the County Court, have done so for the laet decade. The disused chapel in Bank-street,and the old Infirmary in Butterinarket-street were, to state it mildly,far from suitable for the purposes to which they have been put, hut at last all requirements have been met in a most adequate manner. A few days ago an " Examiner " representative availed himself of the courteous invitation of el r E R. Hudson, (clerk of the works), to make an in- spection of the new premises. The details which were gathered from Mr. Hudson are the tour com- menced, give as the facts which we now proceed to enumerate. The work of building commenced at the beginning of August 1896, and proceeded satisfactorily, and expeditiously untlil the autumn was advanced, when considerable delay was rendered unavoidable by the heavy wet weather, and subsequently by the intermittent delivery of I stone. The latter is a special haid kind which was required for the steps and landings in the building. Since then the operations have gone on with as much smoothness as could be expected, and the " finished article " has been at the service of the Inland Revenue authorities for two or three weeks, and of the County Court officials this week. Mr. Henry Tanner, F.R I.R.A. (Her Majesty's Office of Works, Whitehall Place. London) is the architect, and it may be interesting to many to know that the new County Court is built on much the same lines as the one at Derby. Messrs. Beckett and Co., of Hartford, have carried out the building contract in a style which has won wide praise and given the greatest satisfaction. To proceed to statistics, the total area covered by the joint offices—which are only separated by internal arrangements—is almost 900 square yds.lt has two frontages,on i e n Hanover- at., and the other in Wi teigh-sto which measure respectively 80 ft. and 100 It. There are four stories —one a semi-basement—and the total height of the structure is 63 feet. The aggregate cost of its erec- tion is about £9,ooo,which is an exceedingly modest sum considering the excellence of everything con- nected with even the smallest details. A great point of the building is the admirable manner in which the internal arrangements have been planned. The two departments are entirely distinct trone one another and can bis quite shut off from inter-communication. Another good feature from an architectural rolut of view, as well as from that of comfort and convenience,is that though the site Is a square one the Moms are as well lighted in the middle as on the outside, due, of course, to the " well " in the centre of the building. The plauning of the staircases too is most excellent, and an indi- cation ofstheir general commodiousness is given by the fact that the principal one leading to the Court rooms is five feet wide. Another rather interesting feature is that a great many internal partitions, including the whole building, are fire- I proof. Where they are not actually solid brickwork there is a mixture of cement. and con- crete with steel uprights. The whole of the flooring is of solid wood block pr quet —rather a novelty in its way. American maple is used for this purpose, I instead of pitchpine,owing to its superior hardness. Four "Simplex" lire extinguishers and a number of fire buckets are stowed in different parts of the building. The heating arrangements have been car- ried out by Mr, S. Saunders, of Manchester, and are on the low pressure hot water system, consisting of 2-inch pipes with American radiators. The whole system is divided into three circuits, and each radiator is fitted with a valve which admits of the heat being shut off from certain portions of the building when necessary. There are three entrances to the premises, viz., public entrance to the County Court in Hanove et; public entrance to the Inland Revenue Offices .0 Winmarleigh-st; and a private entrance for the judge and officials in Winnearleigh- st. The exterior of the building is composed ot Ed- wards'presseis brick facings,and terracotta dressings. Mr. Hudson led the ." Examiner reporter through the whole of the 27 rcoms of the building, includ- ing the caretaker's suite, but exclusive of cellars, washhouses and lavatories. Frederick Marshall is the name of the caretaker, and he is a retired coast guardsman and has had 30 years experience in the navy. A more typical British sailor it would be insporeible to find. lie and his wife and daughter are established on the top floor of all. The Regis. trees Court is the largest apartment, its dimensions being 421 t. 6 inches long, SOft wide, and 24 feet high. Next to .it comes the Judge's Court which is practi elly a square, being 29ft long and 28 feat wide. Both courts have benches for the public on the floor, the Judge and Registrar being accommodated on a platform. They are two very handsome, comfoi table rooms, and a great contrast to their predecessors, The Judge, Registrar, Jury, Bailiff and Barristers have private rooms. Mention should also be made of the unusually commodious corridor outside the Registrar's Court, which is about 50 feet long and will b 3 supplied with move- able benches. Mr. R. Johnson, Bridge-street, has furnished the offices and other rooms throughout, massive polished oak forming the principal wood used. The doors are of polished teak. None of the walls are papered but are either left white or colour-washed. Pneumatic bells have been fitted to certain portions of the pre- mises. Both departments are proviaed with immense store rooms and coal cellars in the basement,and gas is the illuminant throughout. In several rooms are to be noticed a particularly neat I glazed-bricked fireplace and these have been sup- I plied by the Burmantoffe Faince Works Co., Leeds. The foreman of the works, Mr. William Hollings- worth, who has been responsible for the practical working of everything, is deserving of high praise for his management and supervision during the past eighteen months. A visitor's mind is impressed forcibly by the general air of comfort,commodiousness and bright- ness which the new premises possess. All the rooms are lot ty,and possessing large windows which allow a maximum of light to pervade everywhere. From basement to roof there is nothing wanting, and the handsome appearance of the building externally and inteenally speaks volumes for the way in which the £9,000 has been spent. The first Court will be held on the 31st inst. when the public will have an opportunity of seeing and admiring for thew.- selves.

STOCKTON HEATH.

DEATH OF MAS.JOHN EfEDGCOCIL—Mrs.John Hecigcock wife of Mr. John Hedgcock, of The Grange, Stockton Heath, died at an early hour on Tuesday morning. She was 62 years of age,and had been ailing for a consider- able time, and it was known several weeks ago that her recovery was impossible. She had made a large circle of friends, and her death will be generally regretted by them. The funeral took place on Thursday afternoon in Hill Cliffe graveyard, the Rev.J.S. Hughes perform- ing the ceremony.

EA.RLESTOWN WOMEN'S LIBERAL

ASSOCIATION.

In connection with this flourishing association a most succes3fut social evening was held on Thursday night, in the Town Hall, Earlestown. There was a very large attendance of the fair sex, and the gathering was pre- sided over by Mr. G. W. Rawlins, of Rainhill. The affair was devoid of any polit:cal significance, the idea being to bring as many ladi,s together in a sociable manner, and at the same time assist the fund A of the association. The Chairman in opening the pro. (sidings spoke in a humorous strain of the manner in which the women were following the men, still, he contended, that the result attained by po.itical women was the furtheranci! of the true interests of the country. There were many ways in which men and women could work together,and do much more good than singly. He hoped these asso- ciations would increase and multiply. One of the things women wanted was a vote. They had it in municipal, and parish council elections, &c., but not Parlia- mentary. And why? Supposing they were duly qualified. They wanted first of all to make up their minds to have it. As an illustration he might mention that a lady was recently canvassed for her vote in the London County Council election, and in reply to the canvasser she said

• . she had been a respectable woman all her tile, and she was not going to vote now. (Laughter). He hoped the ladies of Earlestown were not of that way of thinking. Since 105 there had been 31 bye-elections, previous to which the Liberals held 13,the Tories having a majority of 5. In these divisions now the Liberals held 20, and Conservatives 11, making a Liberal majority of 9. These were 'representative seats comprising all classes of con- atituencies. The Liberals hao only lest one, that was York, and it might not be lost ar ..r all. (Hear, hear.) He was sorry to announce that M•aa ilarosey was not able to be present. She had recently become organising Secretary for the Association and she was now engaged at Maidstone in connection with it. An excellent programme of songs, duets, &1., was after- , wards submitted, as follows r—Quartette, " Where are you going my pretty maid," Misses Barr, Berry, Messrs. Clare and Webster ; song, " Kathleen Mavourneen," Miss Smith ; duet, "Spider and Fly," Miss Smith and Mr. Webster ; trio," Care killed the Cat," Messrs. Kidd, Clare,and Webster; song, " May Morning," Miss Barr ; song, "I look for thee in every sower," Mr. F. P. Clare ; quartette, " There was an old woman," Misses Barr. Berry, Messrs. Clare and Webster. elections were also played by the Phantasmagoria band,under the conductorship of Dar. J. Anderton, their contributions being " Hurrah, hurrah, tor England," " Men of Fier- lech," Killarney," " Pop goes the Weasel," " Ash Grove,"" Rule Britannia," and " God save the Queen." A vote of thanks to all those who had talrin part in the gathering was heartily aclorded on th 3 =lien of Mr. Smith, seconded by Mr. 0 wea.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

One experience of being knocked out of the cup ties in the first round strikes me as being more than sufficient. One could hardly,; couple of months ago,have imagined how much interest it would take from the doings of the loos' fifteen. Friendly matches seem an exceedingly poor substitute for the excitements of league battles,and especially also when your team cannot even win these unimportant contests. To fill up the vacancies in the fixture list Warrington have arranged to play Manning- Llano home and away ou successive Saturdays, and the first of the two encounters took place at Valley Parade last Saturday. 1 understand that at least one spectator went, so that interest in the doings of the club cannot be dead yet, All the available strength Warrington had was whipped up,and a full team made the long journey. An experiment was tried in putting Duckworth in the centre and Bate, junior, tehind the scrimmage with his brother. Warrington had the best of a pleasant game all through but instead of winning as any other team ' would have done, they came home with a adverse score of 8 points to two.

This ie nothing new. A few clubs have been made to feel themselves a beaten team on play and yet triumphed by superior scoring power when an opportunity pre- sented itself. For a full three-fourths of the game at Manningham the home team were defending as hard as they knew how, and yet they crossed the Warrington line on two occasions. That heavy-weight, combination ferward-threequarter-half-back Barraclough did the trick on both occasions, and before half-time. The first try was the mutt of good passing initiated by the scorer, and the second was gained by a purely individual effort on his part when near the line. It was tantalising. to see the way in which the visitore threw away their chances. Moores fumbled with the ball when ha had the line at his rnercv,and then Hallam had a beautiful open. log made for him by his captain, and instead of going straight over dodged about until he was captured on the wrong side of the line. The two Bates had really hard hack in their at:emote at scoring. Warrington were des- 1 ti vied not to be nilled however, far in the second half 1 Hallam by a tremendous drop-kick from near touch toweled the Mauningharn goal, Goldeess knows !, he'd tried often eneugh and at last success has attended Hal- lam'a efferts.

The Warrington forwards again played a splendid game, and the backs were all good at times, but their combination was worse than ever. Though the home team was constituted in a strange fashion as an experi- ment their efforte were much more concerted, and resul- ted in a tangible result. The Manningharn correspon- dent of the " Athletic News " has the following remarks lon the play of the Warrington team. "As already I stated, the run of the play was entirely in the visitors' favour, and they had many opportunities which were allowed to go by without good arising therefrom. Indi• I vidually they did well, but their defeat is explained by an almost entire absence of combination in their work and their miserable passing. The game was in their hands had they gone about their work hut they did not. and so they were kept at bay." " Entire absence of combination," and " mist able passing,"-- note that ye Warrington committee ! A candid opinion from an outsider !

The Warrington Committee have chosan the same team to do duty against Manninghtm to-day, but al- tered the three-quarter lino by placing Isherwood and Monies on the wing,and Hallam and Duckworth in tha centre. This alteration does not appear to have any merits. If the two best rnen,lsherwoocl and Moorea, are on the wing they will be in the positions where they will have least to do. Their late full hack,Lorimer, won the gern". for them by a grand goal kick.

In Lancashire, Oldham If 4ve further evidence lof their ability by defeating Runcorn, and as Salford had no difficulty in disposing of Altrincham, Cheshire is now without a solitary representative. The pretensions ?f Barton were upset by Bromley, and poor Fleetwood said good-bve to the cup in very summary fashion at Hull, where the" dark " team of the competition amused themselves by running up thirty-one points to nothing. Widnes found it too difficult a task to win at Leigh,but in the re-ployed tie on Wednesday at Widnes, they simply pulverised their Opponents and won by 2 goals,6 tries, (22 points) to Leigh one try (3 points).

WARRINGTON BOARD 3F GUARDIANS

ELECTIONS.

These elections will take place on April 4th. In the rural districts the poll will be by ballot from 12 to eight, and in Warrington from eight to eight. In the rural districts the poll will be declared the same evening, while with iegard to Warrington it is proposed to count the votes on the morning of the Sth. At noon on Tuesday which was the latest; time at which withdrawals could be received it was found that several who had been nominated had withdrawn. Mr. George Thompson who has been a most useful member of the Board for a number of yeamin order to avoid a contest withdrew his name, This allowed Mr. T. H. Ashcroft,of Sankey-st.,to be returned unopposed far St. Austin's ward, No. 2 division. HOSPITAL RllNDAY.—Special sermons were preached and offertories taken at the Wesleyan Church on Sunday in aid of the Infirmary. The pulpit was occupied morn- ing and evening by the Rev. W. G. Trigge. In the evening the minister preached an appropriate. sermon from the words, " Visit the fatherless and widows in their affliction," James 1.27, and earnest eppe ilwt re made on behalf of the Infirmary' RAILWAY FATALITY AT COLCIMPH.—On Sunday morn- ing Joseph Stretch, an engine-driver in the service the Great Central Railway whilst driving a light engtee from Glazebrook to Wigan noticed the body of a man ' lying on tho line near Culcheth etation.Upon the arrival of the engine-driver at Lowton he informed the station- master who telegraphed to Mr: Walsh the station- master at Culcheth. Mr. Walsh with SOMA of his ass'et- ants walked about half a mile down the line when he came across the dead body. He immediately sent for Dr. Sephton and the police. Dr. Sephtna was of the opinion that the deceased had been dead some hours be- fore *dug found. 1 lie body which was then unknown was removed by P.C. Alexandra to the Harrow Inn to await an inquest. Upon the police making enquiries the body was identified as that of Albert Edward Barnett, shipping salesman at Manchester, 24 years of age, who resided with his permits at 157, Liverpool-road, Cadis- head. At the inquest on Tuesday, the jury returned a I verdict of "Accidental death." They were of the opinion that deceased had fallen asleep in the train and upon waking up and fieding the train moving slowly he jumped from the carriagP.

A shocking act:Went occurred at the works of Messrs. Ludlow Bros., galvanised hollow-ware manufacturers, Palmer-s., Birmingham, on Thursday afternoon. The gale, it is believed, disturbed the equilibrium of the gable end of a building in course of erection, and this, crashing through the roof of the galvanising shop, buried the %crorkrzert Under a MRlii of timber and iron. ' The work of re;cue was carried on in circumstance 9 of coneiderable danger. It was found that four workmen had been killed, and that several had been injured more or less soriourly. About- forty workmen wore in the building at the time, and it was considered marvellous that the leem of life was not RI eater. . .

_ . It is stated that the Poatmaster General ham decided to make a considerable addition to the number of the female clerks employed in connection with the postal establishments. At this moment something like two thousand three hundred females are employed in con- nection with St. Martin's-le-Grand, and before the year is out over a hundred will be added to the Savings Bank establishment alone. The Money Order office, from which female elesks have hitherto been excluded, mow to be thrown open to the sex. An experimental compe- tition is shortly to be held in connection with that office for about thirty positions.

Messrs. Kyaochs,of Birmingham, have for some time pant been engaged in perfecting the mechanism of a new quick-firing gum, and they have succeeded in pro- ducing a weapon a long way ahead of any other gun of a similar type. By turning a wheel the whole mechan- ism is set in working, and six hundred magazine bullets are discharged per minute. The gun,which only weighs .a hundredweight is single-barrelled, and while in action is kept cool by a water-jacket which is filled automati- ' cap. ',the firing action is supplied with cartridges by an endless belt which passes through a box and collects the cartridges. The gun will kill at three miles,and as the barrel is made to swing from right to left it will pro- bably prove to be one of the most deadly weapons of modern warfare.

OUR LOCAL CHIJRCEIEB. --- WYCLIFFE P.S.A. SOCIETY. The meeting ef this society in Wycliffe Church on Sunday afternoon was presided over by Mr. Pickavance. There was a large attendance. In the course of a short address the Chairman referred to two significant inci- dents which nad occurred durinp the previous week. One was the floatation of Messrs. Lipton's Limited when £50,000,000 were subscribed for a subscription list of only £2,500,000, and the other, an account of the dire distress in the West of Ireland, where men women, and children were unable to obtain the necessaries for Subsistence. The Chairman proceeded to refer to this striking contrast of wealth on the one hand and distress and poverty on the other, and pointed out that if only a penny of each pound which , had been subscribed for the floating of Messrs. Lipton's were given to the relief of the Irish distress, the SUM of £208,333 6s. Bd. would be raised. This would be more than sufficient to enable the Irish people to tide over their difficulties. Continuing, the Chairman re- ferred to Christ's command to 6` Bear ye one another's burdens."—The address for the afternoon was delivered by Mr. tSroornhead, of Manchester, who spoke upon character, and referred to Christ, as the greatest ex- ample for them to follow.—Mrs. D. S. Stone contributed a solo entitled "The land of little children," and Mr. R. H. Pearson gave a violin solo. Both items were given with excellent taste, and were greatly appreciated. The service was also taken part in by the Rev. John Yonge, Mr. Healey, and Mr. Whitnall. A contest bweteen English aud French runners took place on Sunday at Ville d'Avray, near Sévres, the former winning easily. Robinson, ot Northampton, was first, followed by Harrison, of Manchester. The runners included men from Salford and elsewhere. During a football match between Alverthorpe and Normanton teams at Alverthorpe on Saturday a player named William Edward Kaye, of Alverthorpe, slipped during a run ard fell with his neck across another play- er's knee. The blow caused such serious injuries ta the spine that death occurred a few hours afterwards.

Whittenbury-Kaye, I have after due consideration, agreed to contest the Ward in the interests of all classes of ratepayers.
